Since $M_\phi$ is open in $\mathbb C^3$, its coordinates $(z_1,z_2,w)$ are global. The function $$d(z)=\det\frac{\partial(F_1,F_2,F_3)}{\partial(z_1,z_2,w)}(z,0)$$ is holomorphic and nowhere zero on $\mathbb B$.

<a id="map:base-cauchy"></a>
<a id="map:fiber-cauchy"></a>
Fix $z\in\mathbb B$ and put $\delta=1-|z|$. The restriction $F_\ell(\,\cdot\,,0)$ is bounded and holomorphic on the entire base ball. For $j=1,2$, the disk $\{z+\zeta e_j:|\zeta|<\delta\}$ is contained in that ball. Cauchy’s estimate, applied on disks with radii tending to $\delta$, gives $$\label{map:base-cauchy}
 |\partial_{z_j}F_\ell(z,0)|\le B\delta^{-1}.$$ For this fixed $z$, the fiber in $M_\phi$ is the disk of radius $e^{-\phi(z)/2}$. Cauchy’s estimate on this disk gives $$\label{map:fiber-cauchy}
 |\partial_wF_\ell(z,0)|\le B e^{\phi(z)/2}.$$ In particular, the base estimate requires no uniform fiber radius near $z$; it is taken entirely on the zero section.

<a id="map:determinant-bound"></a>
The six terms in the determinant expansion, together with <a href="#map:base-cauchy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="map:base-cauchy">[map:base-cauchy]</a> and <a href="#map:fiber-cauchy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="map:fiber-cauchy">[map:fiber-cauchy]</a>, yield $$|d(z)|\le6B^3\delta^{-2}e^{\phi(z)/2},$$ and hence $$\label{map:determinant-bound}
 \log|d(z)|^2
 \le 2\log6+6\log B+4\log\frac1{1-|z|}+\phi(z).$$ Because $\mathbb B$ is simply connected and $d$ has no zeros, it has a global holomorphic logarithm $\ell$. For example, a primitive of the closed holomorphic one-form $d^{-1}\partial d$, with a suitable constant, satisfies $e^\ell=d$. The holomorphic function $H=2\ell$ has $\operatorname{Re}H=\log|d|^2$, so <a href="#map:determinant-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="map:determinant-bound">[map:determinant-bound]</a> contradicts <a href="#base:no-minorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="base:no-minorant">[base:no-minorant]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-map-obstruction-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-map-obstruction</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#main:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:theorem">1</a>.* Choose $\phi$ from Proposition <a href="#base:existence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="base:existence">2</a>, and then choose $\lambda$ as in Proposition <a href="#metric:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:transfer">3</a>. The domain $M=M_\phi\subset\mathbb C^3$ is contractible and carries the asserted smooth complete Kähler metric with two uniform negative real-sectional bounds. Lemma <a href="#map:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="map:obstruction">4</a> excludes a bounded holomorphic map to $\mathbb C^3$ with nowhere-vanishing Jacobian. A biholomorphism to a bounded domain would be such a map: the chain rule applied to its holomorphic inverse makes its Jacobian invertible everywhere. ◻

</details>

The logarithm argument uses nonvanishing along the entire zero section. It gives no assertion that a bounded map’s determinant vanishes identically. Nor are all bounded holomorphic functions constant: $z_1$ and $z_2$ are nonconstant bounded holomorphic functions on $M_\phi$.

<a id="sec:peaks"></a>
### Separated homogeneous peaks

We begin the construction of the potential in Proposition <a href="#base:existence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="base:existence">2</a>. Its analytic requirement will follow from increasingly negative averages against positive measures that preserve holomorphic means. The following observation explains the role of those measures before we construct them.

Write $S=\{\xi\in\mathbb C^2:|\xi|=1\}$, and let $\sigma$ be its surface measure normalized to have mass one. A probability measure $\mu$ on $S$ *represents holomorphic evaluation at zero* if $$\int_S p(\xi)\,d\mu(\xi)=p(0)
 \quad\text{for every holomorphic polynomial }p.$$

<a id="stmt-peaks-mean-criterion"></a>
<div id="peaks:mean-criterion" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Representing-mean criterion). *Let $u$ be a continuous real function on $\mathbb B$. Suppose there are radii $0<r_N<1$ and representing probabilities $\mu_N$ on $S$ such that $$\int_S u(r_N\xi)\,d\mu_N(\xi)\longrightarrow-\infty.$$ Then no holomorphic function $H$ on $\mathbb B$ and constant $C_H$ satisfy $\operatorname{Re}H\le C_H+u$ on $\mathbb B$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-peaks-mean-criterion"><summary>Proof of stmt-peaks-mean-criterion</summary>

*Proof.* For each fixed $r_N<1$, the Taylor polynomials of $H$ converge uniformly on $r_NS$. The representing identities therefore give $$\int_S\operatorname{Re}H(r_N\xi)\,d\mu_N(\xi)
 =\operatorname{Re}H(0).$$ Integrating the proposed inequality contradicts the assumed limit. ◻

</details>

We will apply this criterion to $u(z)=\phi(z)+4\log(1/(1-|z|))$. The first ingredient is a supply of homogeneous polynomials with many separated peaks. Their uniform bounds will also control the derivatives of the eventual potential.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Projective packing and polynomial bounds

The projective distance and separated-power estimate used here occur in the direct construction of Ryll and Wojtaszczyk (Ryll and Wojtaszczyk 1983, 112 and Lemma 2.7) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[RyllWojtaszczyk1983](#ref-RyllWojtaszczyk1983)]. We give the packing argument with a Gaussian tail and an adjustable spacing parameter, since the density corrections will require both. Related constructions with peaks localized in nonisotropic sphere balls appear in Aleksandrov (Aleksandrov 1983, Lemmas 2–3) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Aleksandrov1983](#ref-Aleksandrov1983)]; the caps used here are invariant under a common phase rotation.

Identify complex lines in $\mathbb C^2$ with points of $\mathbb {CP}^1$. For unit representatives define $$d([\xi],[p])=\sqrt{1-|\langle\xi,p\rangle|^2},$$ where the Hermitian inner product is linear in its first argument. This equals the operator norm distance between the rank-one orthogonal projections onto the two lines: their difference has trace zero and eigenvalues $\pm\sqrt{1-|\langle\xi,p\rangle|^2}$. The triangle inequality for the operator norm therefore proves that $d$ is a metric. We also write $d(\xi,p)$ when the representatives are understood.

<a id="peaks:sphere-measure"></a>
In unitary coordinates with $\xi_1=\langle\xi,p\rangle$, write $$\xi=(e^{i\theta}\cos v,e^{i\gamma}\sin v),
 \qquad 0\le v\le\pi/2.$$ The round volume element is $\cos v\sin v\,dv\,d\theta\,d\gamma$. After normalization and the change of variable $u=\cos^2v$, this gives $$\label{peaks:sphere-measure}
 d\sigma=du\,\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}\frac{d\gamma}{2\pi},
 \qquad 0\le u\le1.$$ In particular, a projective cap $d(\xi,p)<s$ has measure $s^2$ for $0\le s\le1$.

Fix $D_0\ge1$. For an integer $k\ge D_0^2$, choose a maximal $D_0/\sqrt{k}$-separated set of lines, and choose one unit representative $p$ for each line. Let $\mathcal P_k$ denote this set of representatives and $N_k=|\mathcal P_k|$. Define the homogeneous holomorphic polynomial <a id="peaks:polynomial"></a>$$\label{peaks:polynomial}
 P_k(z)=\sum_{p\in\mathcal P_k}\langle z,p\rangle^k.$$ The representatives may be chosen arbitrarily; the estimates below hold for every such choice.

<a id="stmt-peaks-bounds"></a>
<div id="peaks:bounds" class="lemma">

<a id="peaks:count"></a>
<a id="peaks:gaussian"></a>
<a id="peaks:global"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Peak bounds). *There are absolute constants $C,c>0$, independent of $D_0\ge1$, such that $$\label{peaks:count}
 \frac{k}{D_0^2}\le N_k\le\frac{4k}{D_0^2}$$ and, for every $\xi\in S$ and $u\ge0$, $$\label{peaks:gaussian}
 \sum_{\substack{p\in\mathcal P_k\\\sqrt{k}d(\xi,p)\ge u}}
       |\langle\xi,p\rangle|^k\le Ce^{-cu^2}.$$ Consequently, $$\label{peaks:global}
 |P_k(z)|\le C|z|^k\quad(z\in\mathbb C^2),
 \qquad
 \int_S|P_k|\,d\sigma\le\frac{2N_k}{k+2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-peaks-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-peaks-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* Put $\rho=D_0/\sqrt{k}\le1$. Maximality gives a covering by caps of radius $\rho$, and separation gives disjoint interiors for caps of radius $\rho/2$. Their measures yield $1\le N_k\rho^2$ and $N_k\rho^2/4\le1$, proving <a href="#peaks:count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="peaks:count">[peaks:count]</a>.

We also need a local count whose constant does not depend on $D_0$. About the centers within distance $u/\sqrt{k}$ of a fixed line, place caps of radius $1/(3\sqrt{k})$. Their interiors are disjoint, and they lie inside the cap of radius $(u+1/3)/\sqrt{k}$ about that line. Comparing measures bounds their number by $9(u+1/3)^2$ when the latter radius is at most one. Otherwise their number is at most $9k\le9(u+1/3)^2$, by comparison with the whole space. Thus the local count is at most $C(1+u)^2$ in all cases.

Since $$|\langle\xi,p\rangle|^k
 =(1-d(\xi,p)^2)^{k/2}
 \le e^{-kd(\xi,p)^2/2},$$ the contribution from the annulus $n\le\sqrt{k}d(\xi,p)<n+1$ is at most $C(n+2)^2e^{-n^2/2}$. Summing these bounds from $n=\lfloor u\rfloor$ onward proves <a href="#peaks:gaussian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="peaks:gaussian">[peaks:gaussian]</a>: the polynomial factor is absorbed by a weaker Gaussian exponent, and the range $0\le u\le2$ is absorbed by the constant. Setting $u=0$ and using homogeneity gives the first assertion in <a href="#peaks:global" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="peaks:global">[peaks:global]</a>, including points outside the unit ball. Finally, <a href="#peaks:sphere-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="peaks:sphere-measure">[peaks:sphere-measure]</a> gives $$\int_S|\langle\xi,p\rangle|^k\,d\sigma
 =\int_0^1 u^{k/2}\,du=\frac2{k+2}.$$ The triangle inequality proves the stated integral bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Patches and a radial profile

For a fixed $R_0>0$ and a center $p\in\mathcal P_k$, use the adapted coordinates above and put <a id="peaks:patch-definition"></a>$$\label{peaks:patch-definition}
 y=-k\log|\xi_1|,\qquad
 \mathcal U_{k,p}=\{\xi\in S:0\le y<R_0\}.$$ The set $y=0$ is the phase circle through $p$. Every patch is invariant under multiplication of $\xi$ by a common phase.

<a id="stmt-peaks-patches"></a>
<div id="peaks:patches" class="lemma">

<a id="peaks:patch-measure"></a>
<a id="peaks:single-peak"></a>
<a id="peaks:off-patch"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Patch estimates). *Fix $R_0>0$. If $D_0>\max\{1,2\sqrt{2R_0}\}$ and $k\ge\max\{D_0^2,2R_0\}$, the patches $\mathcal U_{k,p}$ are pairwise disjoint. On each patch, $$\label{peaks:patch-measure}
 d\sigma=\frac2k e^{-2y/k}\,dy\,
             \frac{d\theta}{2\pi}\frac{d\gamma}{2\pi},$$ and $$\label{peaks:single-peak}
 |P_k(\xi)-e^{-y}e^{ik\theta}|
 \le C e^{-c(D_0-\sqrt{2R_0})^2}.$$ Outside their union, $$\label{peaks:off-patch}
 |P_k(\xi)|\le Ce^{-cR_0}.$$ The constants $C,c$ can be chosen independently of $R_0,D_0,k$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-peaks-patches"><summary>Proof of stmt-peaks-patches</summary>

*Proof.* The projective radius of a patch is $\sqrt{1-e^{-2R_0/k}}\le\sqrt{2R_0/k}$, so the separation of the centers makes the patches disjoint. Formula <a href="#peaks:patch-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="peaks:patch-measure">[peaks:patch-measure]</a> follows from $|\xi_1|^2=e^{-2y/k}$ in <a href="#peaks:sphere-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="peaks:sphere-measure">[peaks:sphere-measure]</a>.

If $\xi\in\mathcal U_{k,p}$ and $p'\ne p$ is another center, the metric triangle inequality gives $$\sqrt{k}d(\xi,p')\ge D_0-\sqrt{2R_0}.$$ The peak from $p$ is exactly $e^{-y}e^{ik\theta}$. Summing all other peaks by <a href="#peaks:gaussian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="peaks:gaussian">[peaks:gaussian]</a> proves <a href="#peaks:single-peak" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="peaks:single-peak">[peaks:single-peak]</a>. Outside every patch, each center satisfies $$kd(\xi,p)^2\ge k(1-e^{-2R_0/k})\ge R_0.$$ The final inequality uses $k\ge2R_0$ and $1-e^{-v}\ge v/2$ for $0\le v\le1$. Another application of <a href="#peaks:gaussian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="peaks:gaussian">[peaks:gaussian]</a> proves <a href="#peaks:off-patch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="peaks:off-patch">[peaks:off-patch]</a>. ◻

</details>

The density correction will use a radial profile with an exact weighted cancellation. The following choice also supplies the strict inequality needed for the logarithmic tests later.

<a id="stmt-peaks-profiles"></a>
<div id="peaks:profiles" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Radial profiles). *Fix $1<a<2$ and put $\alpha=\log a$. One can choose $0<s_1<1$, smooth real functions $f,b$ on $[0,\infty)$, and $R_0>0$ such that <a id="peaks:profile-negativity"></a>$$\label{peaks:profile-negativity}
 \alpha^2-2s_1(\cosh\alpha-1)<0,$$ <a id="peaks:profile-identities"></a>$$\label{peaks:profile-identities}
 \|f\|_\infty<1,\quad f=-s_1\ \text{on }[0,\alpha],\quad
 \int_0^\infty e^{-y}f(y)\,dy=0,\quad
 b'-b=f,\quad b(0)=0.$$ Both functions vanish on a neighborhood of $[R_0,\infty)$. Moreover, $R_0$ can be chosen so that $|aP_k|\le1/2$ off the patches whenever the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#peaks:patches" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="peaks:patches">7</a> hold.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-peaks-profiles"><summary>Proof of stmt-peaks-profiles</summary>

*Proof.* The strict inequality $2(\cosh\alpha-1)>\alpha^2$ allows a choice $$\frac{\alpha^2}{2(\cosh\alpha-1)}<s_1<1.$$ Since $a<2$, this choice also satisfies $s_1(1-e^{-\alpha})<e^{-\alpha}$. Choose a small $\delta>0$ and a smooth cutoff $\chi$, equal to one on $[0,\alpha]$, between zero and one, and zero for $y\ge\alpha+\delta$. For sufficiently small $\delta$, $$N=s_1\int_0^\infty e^{-y}\chi(y)\,dy
 <e^{-(\alpha+2\delta)}.$$ Choose a smooth compactly supported function $\nu$, supported strictly after $\alpha+2\delta$, with $0\le\nu\le1$, and with $$P=\int_0^\infty e^{-y}\nu(y)\,dy>N.$$ Such a function is obtained by approximating the indicator of a sufficiently long interval beginning just after $\alpha+2\delta$. Then $f=-s_1\chi+(N/P)\nu$ has the required plateau and weighted integral. The two supports are disjoint, so $\|f\|_\infty\le\max\{s_1,N/P\}<1$.

Define $$b(y)=e^y\int_0^y e^{-v}f(v)\,dv.$$ It satisfies $b'-b=f$ and $b(0)=0$. Beyond the support of $f$, the integral is exactly zero by the weighted cancellation, so $b$ also has compact support. Near zero, $b(y)=-s_1(e^y-1)$, which is smooth up to the boundary of the half-line. Smoothness here does not require extending $f$ by zero to negative arguments. Finally choose $R_0$ larger than both supports, and enlarge it further until $aCe^{-cR_0}\le1/2$ in <a href="#peaks:off-patch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="peaks:off-patch">[peaks:off-patch]</a>. ◻

</details>

The plateau $0\le y\le\alpha$ is exactly the region where the model peak $ae^{-y}$ has modulus at least one. For the profiles just constructed, the integral of $\log|1-ae^{-y}e^{it'}|^2$ against $(1+f(y)\cos t')\,dy\,dt'/(2\pi)$ on $[0,R_0]\times[0,2\pi]$ equals $\alpha^2-2s_1(\cosh\alpha-1)$, as calculated in <a href="#tests:model-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:model-sign">[tests:model-sign]</a>. Thus the strict profile inequality prescribes a negative logarithmic integral, while the weighted cancellation will preserve holomorphic moments.

Fix these profiles and $R_0$ from now on. The spacing $D_0$ and the degrees will remain available to be chosen sufficiently large. In particular, enlarging $D_0$ later improves the single-peak approximation without altering the profiles.

<a id="sec:densities"></a>
### Positive representing densities

We now construct the representing probabilities required by Lemma <a href="#peaks:mean-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="peaks:mean-criterion">5</a>. Each step adds an oscillation on the current patches. A radial integration by parts makes every holomorphic moment of that oscillation vanish exactly. The separation between consecutive degrees will ensure that the new density stays positive.

Keep the profiles $f,b,R_0$ from Lemma <a href="#peaks:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="peaks:profiles">8</a> fixed, and put $$c_0=\frac{1-\|f\|_\infty}{4}>0.$$ Fix any $D_0>\max\{1,2\sqrt{2R_0}\}$. For an integer $Q\ge2$ large enough that every degree below meets the patch thresholds, put <a id="density:degrees"></a>$$\label{density:degrees}
 k_j=Q^j,\qquad \ell_0=0,\qquad
 \ell_j=\sum_{l=1}^j k_l\quad(j\ge1).$$ Thus $\ell_{j-1}<k_j$ and $\ell_j<2k_j$. At each degree use any of the separated sets and patches constructed in Section <a href="#sec:peaks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:peaks">3</a>.

<a id="density:recursion"></a>
Start with $W_0=1$. At stage $j$, set $k=k_j$. For each $p\in\mathcal P_k$ let $$S_p(\theta)=W_{j-1}(e^{i\theta}p).$$ To determine the correction, consider a phase mode $e^{is\theta}$ with $|s|\le\ell_{j-1}$, the frequency range maintained below. Multiplication by $e^{ik\theta}$ shifts it to frequency $m=k+s>0$; its conjugate can pair with the holomorphic monomial $\xi_1^m=e^{-my/k}e^{im\theta}$. After canceling the surface-measure factor $e^{-2y/k}$, its radial moment will vanish if its coefficient is $b'-(m/k)b$, because $$e^{-my/k}\left(b'-\frac{m}{k}b\right)
 =\frac{d}{dy}\left(e^{-my/k}b\right),
 \qquad b(0)=b(R_0)=0.$$ The identity $f=b'-b$ therefore prescribes the coefficient $f-(s/k)b$ for this mode. Summing the modes leads to the following definition in the adapted coordinates of $\mathcal U_{k,p}$: $$\label{density:recursion}
 \begin{split}
 A_{j,p}(\xi)
 &=e^{2y/k}\operatorname{Re}\left[
     e^{ik\theta}\left(f(y)S_p(\theta)
               -b(y)\frac{S_p'(\theta)}{ik}\right)\right],\\
 W_j&=W_{j-1}+\sum_{p\in\mathcal P_k}A_{j,p},
 \end{split}$$ where each correction is extended by zero off its patch. The prime on $S_p$ denotes differentiation in $\theta$. This recursion is defined without assuming positivity; that property will be proved for one fixed sufficiently large $Q$.

<a id="stmt-density-smooth"></a>
<div id="density:smooth" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Smoothness of the corrections). *Every $W_j$ defined by <a href="#density:recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:recursion">[density:recursion]</a> is a smooth real function on $S$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-density-smooth"><summary>Proof of stmt-density-smooth</summary>

*Proof.* Proceed by induction, beginning with $W_0=1$. The restriction $S_p$ is a smooth periodic function. To check the central circle of a patch, use the smooth coordinates $$(\theta,\zeta)\longmapsto
       (e^{i\theta}\sqrt{1-|\zeta|^2},\zeta),
 \qquad y=-\frac{k}{2}\log(1-|\zeta|^2).$$ All terms of <a href="#density:recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:recursion">[density:recursion]</a> are smooth in these coordinates, including at $\zeta=0$. They do not depend on the transverse polar angle $\gamma$, which would be singular there. Their dependence on $\theta$ is periodic, so it defines a smooth function along the full central circle. At the outer boundary, $f$ and $b$ vanish in a collar of $R_0$; hence the correction is identically zero in a collar of the patch boundary. Its extension by zero is therefore smooth. Summing the finitely many corrections proves the induction. ◻

</details>

Let $T$ denote the phase derivative on $S$, $$TW(\xi)=\left.\frac{d}{d\omega}W(e^{i\omega}\xi)
                  \right|_{\omega=0}.$$ A horizontal vector $Z\in T_\xi S$ will always mean a *Euclidean unit* real tangent vector satisfying the complex equation $\langle Z,\xi\rangle=0$.

A function has phase bandwidth at most $\ell$ if, on every orbit $\omega\mapsto e^{i\omega}\xi$, it is a trigonometric polynomial with frequencies in $[-\ell,\ell]$. Its orbitwise mean is the constant coefficient of this polynomial.

<a id="stmt-density-main"></a>
<div id="density:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 10** (Uniform representing densities). *There is a constant $K_*>0$, depending only on the fixed profiles $f,b$ and $R_0$, with the following property. For every fixed $D_0>\max\{1,2\sqrt{2R_0}\}$ there is an integer threshold $Q_0$ such that, for every integer $Q\ge Q_0$ and every choice of the separated sets above, the recursion <a href="#density:recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:recursion">[density:recursion]</a> has the following properties for all $j\ge1$:*

1.  *$W_j$ is smooth and strictly positive, and $W_j\,d\sigma$ represents holomorphic evaluation at zero. In particular it is a probability measure.*

2.  *Under global phase rotation, $W_j$ has frequencies only between $-\ell_j$ and $\ell_j$, and its mean on every phase orbit is exactly one.*

3.  *At every point of $S$, <a id="density:uniform-bounds"></a>$$\label{density:uniform-bounds}
     \begin{gathered}
     c_0W_{j-1}\le W_j\le2W_{j-1},\\
     |ZW_j|\le K_*\sqrt{k_j}\,W_j,
     \qquad |TW_j|\le K_*k_jW_j
     \end{gathered}$$ for every unit horizontal vector $Z$.*

*The threshold $Q_0$ may also be enlarged to impose any fixed lower threshold on the degrees $k_j$.*

</div>

We first prove the exact moment and frequency assertions; they hold before positivity is known. The remainder of the theorem, including the order of choices of $K_*$ and $Q_0$, is proved in the next subsection.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Exact moments and phase frequencies

<a id="stmt-density-moments"></a>
<div id="density:moments" class="lemma">

<a id="density:representing"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Moment preservation). *For every stage of <a href="#density:recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:recursion">[density:recursion]</a>, the phase bandwidth of $W_j$ is at most $\ell_j$, its orbitwise mean is one, and $$\label{density:representing}
 \int_S p(\xi)W_j(\xi)\,d\sigma(\xi)=p(0)
 \quad\text{for every holomorphic polynomial }p.$$ Each individual correction has zero integral against every such polynomial, including the constant polynomial.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-density-moments"><summary>Proof of stmt-density-moments</summary>

*Proof.* At stage zero the phase assertions are immediate. The polynomial identity for $\sigma$ follows by global phase averaging: every nonconstant homogeneous holomorphic polynomial has nonzero positive phase frequency.

Assume the assertions through stage $j-1$, and write $k=k_j$. The central restriction has a finite expansion $$S_p(\theta)=\sum_{|s|\le\ell_{j-1}}\widehat S_p(s) e^{is\theta},
 \qquad \widehat S_p(0)=1.$$ Since $b'-b=f$, the bracket in <a href="#density:recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:recursion">[density:recursion]</a> is $$\sum_s \widehat S_p(s)\left(b'-\frac{k+s}{k}b\right)e^{is\theta}.$$ All integers $m=k+s$ are positive because $\ell_{j-1}<k$. The patch and $y$ are invariant under global phase rotation, whereas $\theta$ increases by the rotation angle. Hence the correction has frequencies in <a id="density:bandwidth"></a>$$\label{density:bandwidth}
 [k-\ell_{j-1},k+\ell_{j-1}]
 \ \cup\ [-k-\ell_{j-1},-k+\ell_{j-1}].$$ They omit zero. Multiplying by a function of $y$ and extending by zero across the invariant patch boundary do not alter these frequencies. The bandwidth of $W_j$ is thus at most $k+\ell_{j-1}=\ell_j$, and its orbitwise mean remains one.

For the full moment calculation, use an adapted monomial $\xi_1^n\xi_2^l$, with nonnegative integers $n,l$. If $l>0$, averaging in the transverse phase $\gamma$ gives zero. If $l=n=0$, averaging in $\theta$ gives zero because all $m=k+s$ are positive. Suppose then that $l=0$ and $n\ge1$. As $b$ is real, the correction is $$A_{j,p}=\frac{e^{2y/k}}2
 \sum_s\left(\widehat S_p(s) e^{i(k+s)\theta}
              +\overline{\widehat S_p(s)}e^{-i(k+s)\theta}\right)
       \left(b'-\frac{k+s}{k}b\right).$$ Since $\xi_1^n=e^{-ny/k}e^{in\theta}$, only the conjugate frequency with $k+s=n$ can survive the $\theta$ integration. Formula <a href="#peaks:patch-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="peaks:patch-measure">[peaks:patch-measure]</a> gives exactly $$\begin{split}
 \int_{\mathcal U_{k,p}}\xi_1^n A_{j,p}\,d\sigma
 &=\frac{\overline{\widehat S_p(n-k)}}{k}
   \int_0^{R_0}e^{-ny/k}\left(b'-\frac nk b\right)dy\\
 &=\frac{\overline{\widehat S_p(n-k)}}{k}
   \left[e^{-ny/k}b(y)\right]_0^{R_0}=0.
 \end{split}$$ Here a coefficient outside the previous bandwidth is zero. The factor $2/k$ in surface measure combines with the $1/2$ from the real part to leave $1/k$. Both endpoint terms vanish because $b(0)=b(R_0)=0$. The argument applies to every degree $n$, without a degree cutoff. Unitary changes of coordinates preserve holomorphic polynomials, so each correction annihilates every polynomial in the original coordinates. Adding the corrections proves <a href="#density:representing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:representing">[density:representing]</a> and completes the induction. ◻

</details>

In particular, every $W_j$ has total integral one. What remains for Theorem <a href="#density:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="density:main">10</a> is to prove that these exact representing densities are positive with the uniform derivative bounds. The next estimates accomplish this with a single choice of $Q$, independent of the number of stages.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Uniform positivity and derivative estimates

We now prove the remaining assertions of Theorem <a href="#density:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="density:main">10</a>. The main point is to choose one derivative constant $K_*$, followed by one threshold for $Q$, that works at every stage. A phase derivative of the correction in <a href="#density:recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:recursion">[density:recursion]</a> involves $S_p''$. We obtain a bound for this derivative from the frequency support and the estimates already proved at earlier stages.

<a id="stmt-density-orbit-growth"></a>
<div id="density:orbit-growth" class="lemma">

<a id="density:orbit-growth-bound"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Growth along phase orbits). *Suppose $Q\ge2$, $k_j=Q^j$, and the positive functions $W_0=1,W_1,\ldots,W_n$, with $n\ge1$, satisfy $$c_0W_{j-1}\le W_j\le2W_{j-1},
 \qquad |TW_j|\le K_*k_jW_j
 \quad(1\le j\le n).$$ Put $$R=\frac2{c_0},\qquad \beta=\log_2R.$$ For every $p\in S$, the function $S_p(\theta)=W_n(e^{i\theta}p)$ satisfies $$\label{density:orbit-growth-bound}
 S_p(\theta+u)
 \le e^{K_*}R(1+k_n|u|)^\beta S_p(\theta),
 \qquad |u|\le\pi.$$ In particular, the constants in this bound are independent of $Q,n,p$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-density-orbit-growth"><summary>Proof of stmt-density-orbit-growth</summary>

*Proof.* Choose the largest $1\le l\le n$ such that $k_l|u|\le1$, or put $l=0$ if no such index exists. The comparisons between successive densities give $$\frac{W_n(e^{i(\theta+u)}p)}{W_n(e^{i\theta}p)}
 \le R^{n-l}
 \frac{W_l(e^{i(\theta+u)}p)}{W_l(e^{i\theta}p)}.$$ When $l\ge1$, logarithmic differentiation and the phase derivative bound show that the last ratio is at most $\exp(K_*k_l|u|)\le e^{K_*}$. For $l=0$ it equals one. If $l=n$, this already proves the assertion. Otherwise the maximality of $l$, including the case $l=0$, gives $$k_n|u|>Q^{n-l-1}\ge2^{n-l-1}.$$ It follows that $R^{n-l}\le R(1+k_n|u|)^\beta$, proving <a href="#density:orbit-growth-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:orbit-growth-bound">[density:orbit-growth-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-density-weighted-derivative"></a>
<div id="density:weighted-derivative" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (A weighted derivative bound). *Fix $A\ge1$ and $\beta\ge0$. Let $q\ge1$ be an integer, and let $s$ be a positive trigonometric polynomial of degree at most $2q$. Suppose that $$s(\theta+u)\le A(1+q|u|)^\beta s(\theta)
 \qquad(\theta\in\mathbb R,\ |u|\le\pi).$$ There is a constant $C(A,\beta)$, independent of $q,s$, such that $$|s''(\theta)|\le C(A,\beta)q^2s(\theta).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-density-weighted-derivative"><summary>Proof of stmt-density-weighted-derivative</summary>

<a id="density:kernel-decay"></a>
*Proof.* Choose a fixed smooth compactly supported function $\chi:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$ which equals one on $[-2,2]$, and put $$K_q(u)=\sum_{h\in\mathbb Z}\chi(h/q)e^{ihu}.$$ For normalized convolution on the circle, the frequency assumption implies $s''=s*K_q''$. We claim that, for every positive integer $N$, $$\label{density:kernel-decay}
 |K_q''(u)|\le C_Nq^3(1+q|u|)^{-N},
 \qquad |u|\le\pi,$$ where $C_N$ depends only on $N,\chi$.

Indeed the coefficients of $K_q''$ are $$a_h=-h^2\chi(h/q)=q^2g(h/q),
 \qquad g(v)=-v^2\chi(v).$$ There are $O(q)$ nonzero coefficients, each of size $O(q^2)$, so direct summation gives $|K_q''|\le Cq^3$. An $N$-th finite difference of $a_h$ is an iterated integral of the $N$-th derivative of $q^2g(v/q)$, and hence has size at most $C_Nq^{2-N}$. Only $O_N(q)$ such differences can be nonzero, since $g$ is compactly supported and $q\ge1$. Their absolute sum is therefore at most $C_Nq^{3-N}$. Multiplication of the Fourier series by $(1-e^{iu})^N$ produces these differences, up to an index shift. Using $|1-e^{iu}|\ge2|u|/\pi$ on $[-\pi,\pi]$ gives the second estimate $$|K_q''(u)|\le C_Nq^{3-N}|u|^{-N}\qquad(u\ne0).$$ Combining it with the direct estimate proves <a href="#density:kernel-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:kernel-decay">[density:kernel-decay]</a>.

Choose an integer $N>\beta+1$. Positivity, the assumed growth bound, and <a href="#density:kernel-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:kernel-decay">[density:kernel-decay]</a> now give $$\begin{split}
 |s''(\theta)|
 &\le\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}s(\theta-u)|K_q''(u)|
                         \,\frac{du}{2\pi}\\
 &\le AC_Nq^3s(\theta)
       \int_{-\pi}^{\pi}(1+q|u|)^{\beta-N}\,\frac{du}{2\pi}\\
 &\le C(A,\beta)q^2s(\theta).
 \end{split}$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="density:central-derivatives"></a>
Combining these two lemmas with the bandwidth assertion of Lemma <a href="#density:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="density:moments">11</a> gives the following consequence. If the estimates of Theorem <a href="#density:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="density:main">10</a> hold through stage $j-1$, then for $k=k_j$ and the central function $S_p$ in <a href="#density:recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:recursion">[density:recursion]</a>, $$\label{density:central-derivatives}
 \frac{|S_p'|}{kS_p}\le\frac{K_*}{Q},
 \qquad
 \frac{|S_p''|}{k^2S_p}\le\frac{C_1}{Q^2},$$ where $C_1$ depends only on $K_*,c_0$ and the fixed cutoff $\chi$. It is independent of $Q,j,p$. For $j\ge2$, apply the lemmas with $q=k_{j-1}$, noting that $\ell_{j-1}\le2k_{j-1}$; the first inequality follows directly from the phase derivative bound. For $j=1$, both derivatives vanish because $S_p=1$.

<a id="density:patch-directions"></a>
We next estimate a single correction using only normalized bounds. This will allow us to choose $K_*$ before requiring <a href="#density:central-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:central-derivatives">[density:central-derivatives]</a> to be small. All horizontal vectors $Z$ below have Euclidean length one. Enlarge a constant $D_R\ge1$, depending only on $R_0$, so that on every patch at every sufficiently large degree $k$, $$\label{density:patch-directions}
 |Yy|\le D_R,\qquad |Y\theta|\le D_R/k,\qquad
 |Y(e^{ik\theta})|\le D_R,
 \quad Y=Z/\sqrt{k}\ \hbox{or}\ Y=T/k.$$ Here is a direct verification. With $c=\langle\xi,p\rangle$, complex horizontality gives $$|Zc|\le\sqrt{1-|c|^2}
 \le\sqrt{2R_0/k},\qquad |c|\ge e^{-R_0/k}.$$ Differentiate $y=-k\log|c|$ and $\theta=\arg c$ to obtain the first two bounds for $Z/\sqrt{k}$; the third follows from the second. For $T/k$, one has $Yy=0$ and $Y\theta=1/k$. The fixed lower threshold for $k$ makes $e^{-R_0/k}\ge1/2$. We also choose $D_R$ large enough to bound the length of the radial arc from $e^{i\theta}p$ to each patch point by $D_R/\sqrt{k}$. In adapted coordinates this arc is $$u\longmapsto(e^{i\theta}\cos u,e^{i\gamma}\sin u),
 \qquad 0\le u\le \arccos(e^{-y/k}).$$ It has unit speed and is complex-horizontal. The length bound follows from $0\le y\le R_0$ and $1-e^{-y/k}\le R_0/k$. Fix this enlarged $D_R$ throughout the rest of the proof.

<a id="stmt-density-normalized-patch"></a>
<div id="density:normalized-patch" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (Normalized correction estimate). *Write $$F=\|f\|_\infty,\quad B=\|b\|_\infty,\quad
 F_1=\|f'\|_\infty,\quad B_1=\|b'\|_\infty.$$ On a degree-$k$ patch suppose $W_{j-1}>0$, $S_p>0$, and $$\frac{e^{2y/k}S_p}{W_{j-1}}\le2,\qquad
 r_1=\frac{|S_p'|}{kS_p}\le1,\qquad
 r_2=\frac{|S_p''|}{k^2S_p}\le1.$$ For the correction $A_{j,p}$ in <a href="#density:recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:recursion">[density:recursion]</a>, $$|YA_{j,p}|\le C_2W_{j-1},
 \quad Y=Z/\sqrt{k}\ \hbox{or}\ T/k,
 \qquad
 C_2=2D_R\bigl[4(F+B)+F_1+B_1\bigr].$$ In particular, $C_2$ is independent of $K_*,Q,j,p$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-density-normalized-patch"><summary>Proof of stmt-density-normalized-patch</summary>

*Proof.* Besides <a href="#density:patch-directions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:patch-directions">[density:patch-directions]</a>, use $$|Y(e^{2y/k})|\le(2D_R/k)e^{2y/k},\qquad
 |YS_p|\le(D_R/k)|S_p'|,\qquad
 |YS_p'|\le(D_R/k)|S_p''|.$$ Differentiating <a href="#density:recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:recursion">[density:recursion]</a> and taking absolute values gives $$|YA_{j,p}|
 \le D_Re^{2y/k}S_p
 \left[(1+2/k)(F+Br_1)+F_1+B_1r_1+Fr_1+Br_2\right].$$ Since $k\ge1$, the assumed normalized bounds imply the stated estimate. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-density-normalized-patch-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-density-normalized-patch</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#density:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="density:main">10</a>.* Fix $K_*>2C_2/c_0$. The constant $C_1$ in <a href="#density:central-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:central-derivatives">[density:central-derivatives]</a> is now fixed as well. We give a finite list of requirements on $Q$, all independent of the induction stage.

<a id="density:central-comparison"></a>
The horizontal derivative estimate at stage $j-1$, integrated along the radial arc just described, will imply $$\label{density:central-comparison}
 e^{-K_*D_R/\sqrt Q}
 \le\frac{S_p(\theta)}{W_{j-1}(\xi)}
 \le e^{K_*D_R/\sqrt Q}.$$ Indeed the logarithmic derivative along a unit horizontal arc is at most $K_*\sqrt{k_{j-1}}$, and the arc length is at most $D_R/\sqrt{k_j}$. At the initial stage the ratio is exactly one. Since $k_j\ge Q$, put $$H(Q)=\exp\left(K_*D_R/\sqrt Q+2R_0/Q\right).$$

<a id="density:Q-conditions"></a>
Choose a threshold $Q_0$ such that every integer $Q\ge Q_0$ satisfies all earlier degree and patch requirements, and also $$\label{density:Q-conditions}
 \begin{gathered}
 Q\ge2,\qquad H(Q)\le2,\qquad
 K_*/Q\le1,\qquad C_1/Q^2\le1,\qquad
 Q^{-1/2}\le c_0/2,\\
 H(Q)(F+BK_*/Q)\le1-2c_0.
 \end{gathered}$$ These conditions are compatible: $H(Q)\to1$ and $F=1-4c_0<1-2c_0$.

We now fix any integer $Q\ge Q_0$ and induct on $j$. Assume the claimed estimates through stage $j-1$; at $j=1$ the preceding density is the constant one. Equation <a href="#density:central-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:central-derivatives">[density:central-derivatives]</a>, obtained from earlier stages only, and the horizontal comparison <a href="#density:central-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:central-comparison">[density:central-comparison]</a> establish the normalized hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#density:normalized-patch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="density:normalized-patch">14</a>. Moreover, $$|A_{j,p}|
 \le e^{2y/k}S_p(F+Br_1)
 \le H(Q)(F+BK_*/Q)W_{j-1}
 \le(1-2c_0)W_{j-1}.$$ At any point at most one patch correction is nonzero. Hence $$2c_0W_{j-1}\le W_j\le(2-2c_0)W_{j-1},$$ which proves positivity and the required multiplicative comparison.

For $Y=Z/\sqrt{k_j}$, the previous derivative bound gives $|YW_{j-1}|\le(K_*/\sqrt Q)W_{j-1}$. For $Y=T/k_j$, it gives the smaller coefficient $K_*/Q$. Together with Lemma <a href="#density:normalized-patch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="density:normalized-patch">14</a>, <a href="#density:Q-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:Q-conditions">[density:Q-conditions]</a>, and the choice of $K_*$, these yield $$|YW_j|\le(C_2+K_*/\sqrt Q)W_{j-1}
 \le K_*c_0W_{j-1}\le K_*W_j.$$ Outside the patches the correction vanishes. Its smooth zero extension at their boundaries gives the same estimate there. Multiplying by $\sqrt{k_j}$ or $k_j$ proves the two derivative bounds and completes the induction.

Lemma <a href="#density:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="density:moments">11</a> supplies the exact moments, rotation mean and bandwidth independently of these inequalities. In particular $\int_SW_j\,d\sigma=1$; positivity therefore makes $W_j\,d\sigma$ a probability measure. ◻

</details>

We emphasize the order of the choices. The normalized correction constant $C_2$ depends only on the fixed patch data. It determines $K_*$; this in turn determines $C_1$; finally $Q_0$ is chosen. No lower bound for $W_j$ uniform in $j$ is required. The positive comparison at each stage suffices for logarithmic differentiation, and all later integral estimates will use the probability normalization.

<a id="sec:tests"></a>
### Regularized logarithmic tests

Keep the profile parameters $a,\alpha,s_1,f,b,R_0$ from Lemma <a href="#peaks:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="peaks:profiles">8</a>. We shall construct a smooth plurisubharmonic function at each degree whose average against its representing density is uniformly negative. The same negative bound will hold against all later densities. The estimates are uniform for all sufficiently large integers $Q$, so the final value of $Q$ can be chosen after the regularization and spacing parameters.

A positive measure representing holomorphic evaluation need not satisfy the logarithmic inequality $\log|f(0)|\le\int_S\log|f|\,d\mu$ for every holomorphic $f$ continuous on the closed ball. Hedenmalm gives an explicit example of this distinction between representing and Jensen measures (Hedenmalm 1989, Theorem 3.7) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Hedenmalm1989](#ref-Hedenmalm1989)]. Here we need smooth logarithmic tests and negative bounds that persist through all later density corrections. We prove these properties directly for the densities of Theorem <a href="#density:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="density:main">10</a>.

Fix a constant $C_P$ in the bound $|P_k(z)|\le C_P|z|^k$ of Lemma <a href="#peaks:bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="peaks:bounds">6</a>.

<a id="tests:hessian"></a>
<a id="tests:lipschitz"></a>
For $\epsilon>0$, define <a id="tests:regularized"></a>$$\label{tests:regularized}
 F_\epsilon(v)=
 \log\frac{|1-av|^2+\epsilon^2}{1+\epsilon^2},
 \qquad U_k(z)=F_\epsilon(P_k(z)).$$ In particular $F_\epsilon(0)=U_k(0)=0$. The function $U_k$ is smooth and plurisubharmonic on $\mathbb C^2$, since $$\label{tests:hessian}
 (U_k)_{i\bar j}
 =\frac{a^2\epsilon^2}
        {(|1-aP_k|^2+\epsilon^2)^2}
   (P_k)_i\overline{(P_k)_j}.$$ For fixed $\epsilon$ and $A<\infty$, all real derivatives of $F_\epsilon$ of any fixed order are bounded on $|v|\le A$. The mean-value estimate also gives $$\label{tests:lipschitz}
 |F_\epsilon(v)|\le C_{\epsilon,A}|v|
 \qquad (|v|\le A).$$ All subsequent regularization constants may depend on the fixed $\epsilon$.

<a id="stmt-tests-averaging"></a>
<div id="tests:averaging" class="lemma">

<a id="tests:averaging-identity"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Exact averaging). *Let $k=k_j=Q^j$, where $Q$ satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#density:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="density:main">10</a>. For $0\le r\le1$, $$\label{tests:averaging-identity}
 \int_S U_k(r\xi)W_j(\xi)\,d\sigma
 =
 \int_S U_k(r\xi)\,d\sigma
 +\sum_p\int_{\mathcal U_{k,p}}
     U_k(r\xi)e^{2y/k}f(y)\cos(k\theta)\,d\sigma.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tests-averaging"><summary>Proof of stmt-tests-averaging</summary>

*Proof.* Under the global phase rotation $\xi\mapsto e^{i\omega}\xi$, homogeneity gives $P_k(e^{i\omega}\xi)=e^{ik\omega}P_k(\xi)$. Thus $U_k(r\xi)$ has only phase frequencies in $k\mathbb Z$. By Lemma <a href="#density:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="density:moments">11</a>, the phase bandwidth of $W_{j-1}$ is $\ell_{j-1}<k$ and its constant coefficient on each orbit is one. Averaging the product over the phase therefore gives $$\int_S U_k(r\xi)W_{j-1}(\xi)\,d\sigma
 =\int_S U_k(r\xi)\,d\sigma.$$

Each patch is phase-invariant. Its radial variable $y$ is fixed under rotation, while $\theta$ and $\gamma$ both increase by $\omega$. Write the central restriction as $S_p(\theta)=W_{j-1}(e^{i\theta}p)
 =\sum_s\widehat S_p(s)e^{is\theta}$. In the patch increment of <a href="#density:recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="density:recursion">[density:recursion]</a>, the frequencies are $\pm(k+s)$ with $|s|\le\ell_{j-1}<k$. Pairing with a frequency in $k\mathbb Z$ is possible only for $s=0$. Since $\widehat S_p(0)=1$ and $S_p'$ has zero constant coefficient, the surviving part of the increment is exactly $e^{2y/k}f(y)\cos(k\theta)$. Integrating this phase average proves <a href="#tests:averaging-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:averaging-identity">[tests:averaging-identity]</a>. ◻

</details>

The next estimate chooses the regularization before the spacing. This order matters: the single-peak approximation will be applied to a fixed smooth logarithm, and its error can then be made small by increasing $D_0$.

<a id="stmt-tests-negative"></a>
<div id="tests:negative" class="proposition">

<a id="tests:one-scale-negative"></a>
**Proposition 16** (Negative averages at one scale). *There exist $\epsilon>0$, $\eta\in(0,1/2)$ and $D_*\ge1$, depending only on the fixed profile data, with the following property. For every $D_0\ge D_*$ there are $k_*>0$ and $d_2>0$ such that, for every integer $Q$ satisfying the density theorem and $Q\ge k_*$, $$\label{tests:one-scale-negative}
 \int_S U_{k_j}(r\xi)W_j(\xi)\,d\sigma\le-d_2
 \qquad
 \bigl(j\ge1,\quad 1-\eta\le r^{k_j}\le1\bigr).$$ The constants $k_*,d_2$ may depend on $D_0$, but are independent of $j$ and of further increases in $Q$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tests-negative"><summary>Proof of stmt-tests-negative</summary>

<a id="tests:model"></a>
*Proof.* Put $$\Delta=2s_1(\cosh\alpha-1)-\alpha^2>0.$$ We first compute the integral for a single peak: $$\label{tests:model}
 I_\epsilon(\rho)=
 \int_0^{R_0}\int_0^{2\pi}
 \log\frac{|1-a\rho e^{-y}e^{it'}|^2+\epsilon^2}
               {1+\epsilon^2}
 (1+f(y)\cos t')\,\frac{dt'}{2\pi}\,dy.$$ At $\rho=1$, the unregularized logarithm has only one singularity, at $(y,t')=(\alpha,0)$ modulo $2\pi$. Near that point, $$|1-e^{\alpha-y+it'}|^2
 \asymp (y-\alpha)^2+(t')^2.$$ Its logarithm is locally integrable. For $0<\epsilon\le1$, the regularized logarithm is bounded above uniformly, and its negative part is bounded by the negative part of the unregularized logarithm plus $\log2$. Dominated convergence therefore applies in <a href="#tests:model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:model">[tests:model]</a>.

<a id="tests:model-sign"></a>
For $q<1$, the mean and cosine moment of $\log|1-qe^{it'}|^2$ are $0$ and $-q$, respectively, by the logarithm series. For $q>1$, factoring out $q^2$ gives mean $2\log q$ and cosine moment $-q^{-1}$. Using the cancellation $\int_0^{R_0}e^{-y}f(y)\,dy=0$, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 I_0(1)
 &=\alpha^2-\int_0^\alpha f(y)e^{y-\alpha}\,dy
             -\int_\alpha^{R_0}f(y)e^{\alpha-y}\,dy \notag\\
 &=\alpha^2+\int_0^\alpha
       f(y)\bigl(e^{\alpha-y}-e^{-(\alpha-y)}\bigr)\,dy \notag\\
 &=\alpha^2-2s_1(\cosh\alpha-1)=-\Delta.
 \label{tests:model-sign}
\end{aligned}$$ The last equality uses $f=-s_1$ on $[0,\alpha]$.

<a id="tests:off-patch"></a>
We now compare this model with <a href="#tests:averaging-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:averaging-identity">[tests:averaging-identity]</a>. For $k=k_j$ put $\rho=r^k$, so homogeneity gives $U_k(r\xi)=F_\epsilon(\rho P_k(\xi))$. Off the patches, $|aP_k|\le1/2$ by Lemma <a href="#peaks:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="peaks:profiles">8</a>. If $v=\rho P_k(\xi)$, $0\le\rho\le1$, and $s=|1-av|^2$, then $1/4\le s\le9/4$ and $$F_\epsilon(v)-\log|1-av|^2
 =\log(1+\epsilon^2/s)-\log(1+\epsilon^2).$$ The derivative in $s$ of the first term has magnitude at most $16\epsilon^2$, and $|s-1|\le C|v|$. Hence $$|F_\epsilon(v)-\log|1-av|^2|
 \le C\epsilon^2|P_k(\xi)|.$$ The off-patch set is phase-invariant, and the phase mean of the unregularized logarithm is zero. By the $L^1$ estimate of Lemma <a href="#peaks:bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="peaks:bounds">6</a>, its total contribution is thus at most $$\label{tests:off-patch}
 C_{\rm off}\epsilon^2\,\frac{N_k}{k}.$$ Here $C_{\rm off}$ is independent of $D_0,k,\rho$ and $0<\epsilon\le1$.

<a id="tests:patch-error"></a>
On a patch, the measure formula of Lemma <a href="#peaks:patches" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="peaks:patches">7</a> writes the combined contribution as $$\frac2k\int_0^{R_0}\int_{\mathbb T^2}
 F_\epsilon(\rho P_k(\xi))
       \bigl(e^{-2y/k}+f(y)\cos(k\theta)\bigr)
       \,\frac{d\theta\,d\gamma}{(2\pi)^2}\,dy.$$ For fixed $\epsilon,R_0$, replace $P_k(\xi)$ by $e^{-y}e^{ik\theta}$ using the single-peak estimate, and replace $e^{-2y/k}$ by one. The bounded derivative of $F_\epsilon$ and the bound $|e^{-2y/k}-1|\le2R_0/k$ show that the result differs from $(2/k)I_\epsilon(\rho)$ by at most $$\label{tests:patch-error}
 \frac2k C_{\epsilon,R_0}
 \left(e^{-c(D_0-\sqrt{2R_0})^2}+\frac1k\right).$$ All constants are uniform over patches and $0\le\rho\le1$. The change from $k\theta$ to $t'$ in the angular average is exact, as $k$ is an integer.

Choose $\epsilon\in(0,1]$ so small that $$|I_\epsilon(1)+\Delta|\le\Delta/8,
 \qquad C_{\rm off}\epsilon^2\le\Delta/8.$$ By continuity of <a href="#tests:model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:model">[tests:model]</a> in $\rho$, choose $\eta\in(0,1/2)$ such that $I_\epsilon(\rho)\le-3\Delta/4$ for $1-\eta\le\rho\le1$. Next choose $D_*$ large enough for patch disjointness and for the first error in <a href="#tests:patch-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:patch-error">[tests:patch-error]</a> to be at most $\Delta/16$ inside its factor $2/k$. For each $D_0\ge D_*$, take $k_*$ large enough for all the geometric estimates and for the remaining error inside this factor to be at most $\Delta/16$. Summing the patches and adding <a href="#tests:off-patch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:off-patch">[tests:off-patch]</a> gives, in particular, $$\int_S U_k(r\xi)W_j\,d\sigma
 \le-\frac{\Delta}{2}\frac{N_k}{k}
 \le-\frac{\Delta}{2D_0^2}.$$ Thus $d_2=\Delta/(2D_0^2)$ is a valid choice. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-tests-later"></a>
<div id="tests:later" class="lemma">

<a id="tests:later-negative"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Negative averages against later densities). *Fix $\epsilon,\eta,D_0,k_*,d_2$ as in Proposition <a href="#tests:negative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tests:negative">16</a>, and put $d_3=d_2/2$. There is a threshold $Q_*$ such that, for every integer $Q\ge Q_*$, the densities satisfy $$\label{tests:later-negative}
 \int_S U_{k_j}(r\xi)W_N(\xi)\,d\sigma\le-d_3
 \qquad
 \bigl(N\ge j\ge1,\quad1-\eta\le r^{k_j}\le1\bigr).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tests-later"><summary>Proof of stmt-tests-later</summary>

*Proof.* Include the density and degree thresholds in $Q_*$, and require $Q\ge4$. By Lemma <a href="#density:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="density:moments">11</a>, the increment $W_\nu-W_{\nu-1}$ has frequencies within $\ell_{\nu-1}$ of $\pm k_\nu$. Since $\ell_{\nu-1}\le2k_{\nu-1}$, each such frequency has absolute value at least $(Q-2)k_{\nu-1}$. Summing the increments with $\nu>j$ shows that every frequency in $W_N-W_j$ has absolute value at least $(Q-2)k_j$.

For fixed $\xi,r$, write the logarithmic profile as $$V(t')=\log\frac{|1-Ae^{it'}|^2+\epsilon^2}{1+\epsilon^2},
 \qquad A=a r^{k_j}P_{k_j}(\xi).$$ The uniform polynomial bound gives $|A|\le aC_P$. Differentiating the logarithm shows that $$\|V''\|_\infty\le
 B_\epsilon:=
 \frac{2aC_P}{\epsilon^2}
 +\frac{4a^2C_P^2}{\epsilon^4}.$$ Two integrations by parts bound its $m$th Fourier coefficient by $B_\epsilon/m^2$ for $m\ne0$. To track these coefficients as functions of $\xi$, define $$c_m(\xi)=\int_0^{2\pi}
 F_\epsilon\bigl(r^{k_j}P_{k_j}(\xi)e^{it'}\bigr)
 e^{-imt'}\,\frac{dt'}{2\pi}.$$ Homogeneity and a change of variable give $c_m(e^{i\omega}\xi)=e^{imk_j\omega}c_m(\xi)$. Thus $c_m$ has global phase frequency $mk_j$, and $U_{k_j}(r\xi)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb Z}c_m(\xi)$. The coefficient bound makes this series uniformly convergent. Its truncation to $|m|\le Q-3$ has uniform error at most $2B_\epsilon/(Q-3)$ and only frequencies of absolute value less than $(Q-2)k_j$. Phase averaging therefore makes its integral against $W_N-W_j$ vanish. Both densities are positive probabilities by Theorem <a href="#density:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="density:main">10</a>; hence $\|W_N-W_j\|_{L^1(\sigma)}\le2$. It follows that $$\left|\int_S U_{k_j}(r\xi)(W_N-W_j)\,d\sigma\right|
 \le\frac{4B_\epsilon}{Q-3}.$$ Choose $Q_*$ so that this is at most $d_2/2$, and apply <a href="#tests:one-scale-negative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:one-scale-negative">[tests:one-scale-negative]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:base-potential"></a>
### The controlled base potential

<a id="base:potential"></a>
We now sum the logarithmic tests to prove Proposition <a href="#base:existence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="base:existence">2</a>. Fix parameters and an integer $Q$ for which Theorem <a href="#density:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="density:main">10</a> and Lemma <a href="#tests:later" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tests:later">17</a> hold, and write $k_j=Q^j$. For a finite amplitude $L>0$, set $$\label{base:potential}
 \phi(z)=\psi(z)+L\sum_{j=1}^\infty U_{k_j}(z).$$ We first justify this definition. We then choose $L$ to exclude the required pluriharmonic minorant, and finally prove the uniform chart estimates needed by the metric construction.

<a id="stmt-base-convergence"></a>
<div id="base:convergence" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (Smooth convergence). *For every finite $L>0$, the series in <a href="#base:potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="base:potential">[base:potential]</a> converges locally in $C^\infty(\mathbb B)$. Its sum is smooth, satisfies $\phi(0)=0$, and has complex Hessian $h\ge I$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-base-convergence"><summary>Proof of stmt-base-convergence</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $0<r_0<r_1<1$. Around each point of $|z|\le r_0$, the complex polydisk of radius $(r_1-r_0)/(2\sqrt2)$ lies in $|z|<r_1$. Cauchy estimates there, together with Lemma <a href="#peaks:bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="peaks:bounds">6</a>, give $$\sup_{|z|\le r_0}|D^mP_k(z)|
 \le C_{m,r_0,r_1}r_1^k$$ for every fixed derivative order $m$. Here and below $D^m$ denotes any real partial derivative of total order $m$. The values of $P_k$ remain in a fixed bounded disk. Every positive-order derivative of $F_\epsilon(P_k)$ is a sum of products containing at least one positive-order derivative of $P_k$ or its conjugate. The bounded derivatives of $F_\epsilon$ therefore give the same exponential estimate for $D^mU_k$. For $m=0$, use <a href="#tests:lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:lipschitz">[tests:lipschitz]</a>. Since $\sum_j r_1^{Q^j}<\infty$, the series converges in every local $C^m$ norm.

Each summand vanishes at zero and is plurisubharmonic by <a href="#tests:hessian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:hessian">[tests:hessian]</a>. Termwise differentiation is justified by the local $C^2$ convergence, and hence $$\partial\bar\partial\phi
 =I+L\sum_j\partial\bar\partial U_{k_j}\ge I.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-base-minorant"></a>
<div id="base:minorant" class="lemma">

<a id="base:amplitude"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Absence of a pluriharmonic minorant). *If $$\label{base:amplitude}
 L>\frac{5\log Q}{d_3},$$ there are no $H\in\mathcal O(\mathbb B)$ and $C_H\in\mathbb R$ satisfying <a href="#base:no-minorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="base:no-minorant">[base:no-minorant]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-base-minorant"><summary>Proof of stmt-base-minorant</summary>

<a id="base:tail"></a>
*Proof.* Put <a id="base:radii"></a>$$\label{base:radii}
 r_N=1-\frac{\eta}{k_N}.$$ For every $j\le N$, Bernoulli’s inequality gives $$r_N^{k_j}
 =\left(1-\frac{\eta}{k_N}\right)^{k_j}
 \ge1-\eta\frac{k_j}{k_N}\ge1-\eta.$$ Thus all of the first $N$ modes satisfy the hypothesis of <a href="#tests:later-negative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:later-negative">[tests:later-negative]</a> at this same radius. For $j=N+m>N$, on the other hand, $$r_N^{k_j}\le e^{-\eta k_j/k_N}=e^{-\eta Q^m}.$$ By the uniform polynomial bound and <a href="#tests:lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:lipschitz">[tests:lipschitz]</a>, $$\label{base:tail}
 \sum_{j>N}\sup_{\xi\in S}|U_{k_j}(r_N\xi)|
 \le C_\epsilon\sum_{m\ge1}e^{-\eta Q^m}
 =:C_{\rm tail}<\infty,$$ independently of $N$. Because $W_N$ is a positive probability, the same bound controls the weighted tail, without requiring a uniform pointwise bound on $W_N$.

<a id="base:negative-means"></a>
The base potential contributes only one logarithm: $$\psi(r_N\xi)=-\log(1-r_N^2)
 \le\log\frac1{1-r_N}=\log(k_N/\eta).$$ Combining this with <a href="#tests:later-negative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:later-negative">[tests:later-negative]</a> and <a href="#base:tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="base:tail">[base:tail]</a> yields $$\begin{aligned}
 &\int_S\left(\phi(r_N\xi)
                  +4\log\frac1{1-r_N}\right)W_N\,d\sigma \notag\\
 &\hspace{1cm}\le
 5\log(k_N/\eta)+L(-d_3N+C_{\rm tail}) \notag\\
 &\hspace{1cm}=
 N(5\log Q-Ld_3)+5\log(1/\eta)+LC_{\rm tail}
 \longrightarrow-\infty.
 \label{base:negative-means}
\end{aligned}$$

By Theorem <a href="#density:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="density:main">10</a>, the measures $\mu_N=W_N\,d\sigma$ are positive representing probabilities. Apply Lemma <a href="#peaks:mean-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="peaks:mean-criterion">5</a> to these measures, the radii $r_N$, and the continuous function $u(z)=\phi(z)+4\log(1/(1-|z|))$. The limit <a href="#base:negative-means" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="base:negative-means">[base:negative-means]</a> is exactly its hypothesis, so the required minorant cannot exist. ◻

</details>

It remains to control the metric jets as the chart centers approach the boundary. The value of $\phi$ need not be bounded. For its derivatives, the low degrees become almost constant in a centered chart, while the high degrees are exponentially small.

<a id="stmt-base-chart-estimates"></a>
<div id="base:chart-estimates" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20** (Uniform chart estimates). *For every fixed finite $L>0$, the potential <a href="#base:potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="base:potential">[base:potential]</a> has uniformly bounded positive-order derivatives through order four at the origins of all centered ball charts. Consequently its complex Hessian satisfies <a href="#base:control" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="base:control">[base:control]</a> and <a href="#base:jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="base:jets">[base:jets]</a>, with $c=1$ and a finite constant $C$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-base-chart-estimates"><summary>Proof of stmt-base-chart-estimates</summary>

*Proof.* After a unitary change of coordinates, put the center at $(r,0)$, where $0\le r<1$, and let $\delta=1-r$. Rotating $P_k$ preserves its degree and its uniform homogeneous bound. Use the chart $T_r$ of <a href="#base:charts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="base:charts">[base:charts]</a>, on the fixed complex polydisk $$D_\rho=\{|\zeta_1|<\rho,\ |\zeta_2|<\rho\},
 \qquad \rho=\frac1{16}.$$ The denominators in $T_r$ have modulus at least $1-\rho$. We estimate the polynomial in the two ranges of $k\delta$.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
###### High degrees: $k\delta>1$.

The ball automorphism identity gives, on $D_\rho$, $$1-|T_r(\zeta)|^2
 =\frac{(1-r^2)(1-|\zeta|^2)}{|1+r\zeta_1|^2}
 \ge \frac{1-2\rho^2}{(1+\rho)^2}\,\delta.$$ It follows that $1-|T_r(\zeta)|\ge c\delta$, with a fixed $c>0$. Thus $$|P_k(T_r(\zeta))|\le C_P e^{-ck\delta}.$$ Cauchy estimates in this fixed polydisk bound every fixed-order holomorphic derivative at zero, including order zero, by $C_m e^{-ck\delta}$.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
###### Low degrees: $k\delta\le1$.

Consider the polydisk in the original coordinates $$A_k=\left\{|z_1-r|<\frac1k,\quad
                 |z_2|<\frac1{\sqrt k}\right\}.$$ It may extend outside the ball, but $P_k$ is an entire homogeneous polynomial. The bound of Lemma <a href="#peaks:bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="peaks:bounds">6</a> therefore holds there as well. In fact $$|z|^2\le(1+1/k)^2+1/k\le1+4/k,\qquad
 |P_k(z)|\le C_P(1+2/k)^k\le C_Pe^2.$$ Cauchy estimates on the concentric half-polydisk give $$|\partial_1P_k|\le Ck,\qquad
 |\partial_2P_k|\le C\sqrt k.$$ Meanwhile the chart displacements satisfy $$|T_r^1(\zeta)-r|
 \le\frac{2\rho}{1-\rho}\delta,\qquad
 |T_r^2(\zeta)|
 \le\frac{\sqrt2\rho}{1-\rho}\sqrt\delta.$$ Both numerical coefficients are less than $1/2$. As $\delta\le1/k$, the chart image lies in the half-polydisk just used. Integrating the gradient along the straight segment from $(r,0)$ to $T_r(\zeta)$ gives $$|P_k(T_r(\zeta))-P_k(r,0)|
 \le C(k\delta+\sqrt{k\delta})
 \le2C\sqrt{k\delta}.$$ This segment stays in the half-polydisk by convexity. Apply Cauchy estimates on $D_\rho$ to the holomorphic difference $P_k\circ T_r-P_k(r,0)$. Every positive-order derivative at zero is bounded by $C_m\sqrt{k\delta}$. We make no such assertion for the zeroth-order value in this range.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
###### Composition and summation.

In both ranges the values of $P_k\circ T_r$ stay in a fixed bounded disk. Each positive-order real derivative of $U_k\circ T_r=F_\epsilon(P_k\circ T_r)$ is a sum of products containing differentiated factors of $P_k\circ T_r$ or its conjugate. For $1\le m\le4$, the preceding bounds and the smooth composition estimate give <a id="base:scale-jets"></a>$$\label{base:scale-jets}
 |D^m(U_k\circ T_r)(0)|
 \le C_{\epsilon,m}
 \begin{cases}
  \sqrt{k\delta},&k\delta\le1,\\
  e^{-ck\delta},&k\delta>1.
 \end{cases}$$ Indeed every term contains at least one differentiated factor, and a product of several factors obeys the same bound because the displayed scale factor is at most one. This proves the estimate for all real derivatives, hence for all pure and mixed complex derivatives of these orders.

Let $J$ be the largest index with $Q^J\delta\le1$, or put $J=0$ if there is no such index. The low-degree contribution is bounded by the geometric sum $$\sum_{j\le J}\sqrt{Q^j\delta}
 \le\frac1{1-Q^{-1/2}}.$$ For the remaining degrees, the first value of $Q^j\delta$ exceeds one, so $$\sum_{j>J}e^{-cQ^j\delta}
 \le\sum_{m\ge0}e^{-cQ^m}<\infty.$$ Both bounds are uniform in the chart center. Termwise differentiation is legitimate by Lemma <a href="#base:convergence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="base:convergence">18</a>. Multiplication by the fixed amplitude $L$ preserves these bounds.

Finally, the base potential transforms as $$\psi\circ T_r
 =-\log(1-|\zeta|^2)-\log(1-r^2)
      +\log|1+r\zeta_1|^2.$$ Its positive-order derivatives through order four at zero are uniformly bounded. The last term is pluriharmonic, so the complex Hessian of $\psi\circ T_r$ is exactly the standard ball Hessian in these coordinates. The derivatives of $\phi\circ T_r$ needed for the pulled-back Hessian components and their first and second derivatives have orders two, three and four. The bounds just proved, together with $h\ge I$ from Lemma <a href="#base:convergence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="base:convergence">18</a>, establish the assertion. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-base-chart-estimates-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-base-chart-estimates</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#base:existence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="base:existence">2</a>.* Choose $a,\alpha,s_1,f,b,R_0$ as in Lemma <a href="#peaks:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="peaks:profiles">8</a>. Proposition <a href="#tests:negative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tests:negative">16</a> first chooses $\epsilon,\eta$ and then permits any sufficiently large spacing $D_0$. Fix such a $D_0$ and its degree threshold $k_*$, and fix $d_3=d_2/2>0$. The density theorem holds for every $Q$ above a threshold depending only on the fixed patch data and spacing. Its derivative constant $K_*$ is fixed before that threshold is chosen. We may therefore choose one integer $Q$ satisfying the density threshold, $Q\ge k_*$, and the Fourier-error threshold of Lemma <a href="#tests:later" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tests:later">17</a>.

Now fix a finite amplitude $L$ satisfying <a href="#base:amplitude" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="base:amplitude">[base:amplitude]</a>. Lemma <a href="#base:convergence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="base:convergence">18</a> constructs the smooth potential with $h\ge I$; Lemma <a href="#base:minorant" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="base:minorant">19</a> proves the required obstruction; and Lemma <a href="#base:chart-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="base:chart-estimates">20</a> supplies its finite uniform chart bounds. The chart constant is allowed to depend on these fixed choices, including $L$. The metric parameter $\lambda$ in Proposition <a href="#metric:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:transfer">3</a> is chosen only afterward from these bounds, so none of the choices above depends on $\lambda$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:hartogs"></a>
### The global Hartogs metric

We now turn to Proposition <a href="#metric:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:transfer">3</a>. In this section we prove all its global geometric assertions. The real sectional-curvature bounds will follow from the calculation in Section <a href="#sec:curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:curvature">8</a> and the estimates in Section <a href="#sec:pinching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pinching">9</a>.

The potential specializes Calabi’s construction on a Hermitian line bundle (Calabi 1979, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Calabi1979](#ref-Calabi1979)]. We verify its positivity, smoothness, and completeness directly, including at the zero section.

<a id="stmt-metric-complete"></a>
<div id="metric:complete" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21**. *Let $\phi$ be a smooth real plurisubharmonic function on $\mathbb B$, and let $\lambda>0$. The domain $M_\phi$ in <a href="#metric:domain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="metric:domain">[metric:domain]</a> is contractible, and the complex Hessian of $\mathcal K_\lambda=\lambda\psi-\log(1-e^\phi|w|^2)$ defines a smooth geodesically complete Kähler metric $g=2\operatorname{Re}\widehat g$ on $M_\phi$. Moreover, $\mathcal K_\lambda$ is a smooth strictly plurisubharmonic exhaustion of $M_\phi$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-metric-complete"><summary>Proof of stmt-metric-complete</summary>

*Proof.* *The domain and a smooth positive metric.* The function $t=e^\phi|w|^2$ is smooth on $\mathbb B\times\mathbb C$, so $M_\phi=\{t<1\}$ is open in $\mathbb C^3$. The map $$(z,w)\longmapsto (z,e^{\phi(z)/2}w)$$ is a smooth real diffeomorphism from $M_\phi$ to $\mathbb B\times\mathbb D$, where $\mathbb D=\{u\in\mathbb C:|u|<1\}$. Its inverse is $(z,u)\mapsto(z,e^{-\phi(z)/2}u)$. Thus $M_\phi$ is contractible; no holomorphic trivialization is needed for this conclusion.

<a id="metric:smooth-form"></a>
<a id="metric:axis"></a>
The potential $\mathcal K_\lambda$ is smooth at every point of $M_\phi$, including its zero section. Write $$h=\partial\bar\partial\phi,\qquad
 x=\frac{t}{1-t},\qquad G=\lambda I+xh,
 \qquad \vartheta=dw+w\partial\phi.$$ The base covectors together with $\vartheta$ form a global smooth complex coframe. Differentiating the potential gives $$\label{metric:smooth-form}
 \widehat g
 =G+\frac{e^\phi}{(1-t)^2}\,
       \vartheta\otimes\overline{\vartheta}.$$ For a complex tangent vector $u=(u_z,u_w)$ this means $$\widehat g(u,u)
 =G(u_z,u_z)
  +\frac{e^\phi}{(1-t)^2}
      |u_w+w\partial\phi(u_z)|^2.$$ Since $h\ge0$, the first term controls every nonzero horizontal projection through $G\ge\lambda I$; when $u_z=0$, the second term is positive unless $u_w=0$. Thus $\widehat g$ is positive definite. At the zero section its matrix is particularly simple: $$\label{metric:axis}
 \widehat g\big|_{(z,0)}
 =\lambda I(z)+e^{\phi(z)}\,dw\otimes d\bar w.$$ The mixed blocks vanish. Being a positive complex Hessian of a smooth real potential, $\widehat g$ defines the stated Kähler metric.

Away from the zero section one can write $s=\log t$ and $q=x(1+x)=t/(1-t)^2$. Then $\partial s=\vartheta/w$, so <a href="#metric:smooth-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="metric:smooth-form">[metric:smooth-form]</a> also reads <a id="metric:log-form"></a>$$\label{metric:log-form}
 \widehat g=\lambda I+xh+
             q\,\partial s\otimes\overline{\partial s}.$$ We will use this expression for the curvature computation. Formula <a href="#metric:smooth-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="metric:smooth-form">[metric:smooth-form]</a>, rather than the coordinate $s$, defines the metric across $w=0$.

<a id="metric:gradient-convention"></a>
*Two functions with bounded gradient.* For a real smooth function $f$, the convention $g=2\operatorname{Re}\widehat g$ gives $$\label{metric:gradient-convention}
 |\nabla f|_g^2=2|\partial f|_{\widehat g^{-1}}^2.$$ Indeed, for a real vector $X$ with $(1,0)$ part $u$, $g(X,X)=2\widehat g(u,u)$ and $df(X)=2\operatorname{Re}\partial f(u)$, and the identity follows by taking dual norms.

<a id="metric:gradient-bounds"></a>
Globally on $M_\phi$, $$\partial[-\log(1-t)]
 =\frac{e^\phi\bar w}{1-t}\,\vartheta.$$ In the coframe of <a href="#metric:smooth-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="metric:smooth-form">[metric:smooth-form]</a>, the Hermitian metric is block diagonal. Therefore $$|\partial[-\log(1-t)]|_{\widehat g^{-1}}^2
 =\frac{|e^\phi\bar w/(1-t)|^2}{e^\phi/(1-t)^2}=t.$$ This calculation includes $w=0$, where both sides are zero. For the base potential, direct inversion of its complex Hessian gives $$|\partial\psi|_{I^{-1}}^2=|z|^2.$$ The covector $\partial\psi$ has only base components in the same coframe, and $G\ge\lambda I$. Combining these facts with <a href="#metric:gradient-convention" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="metric:gradient-convention">[metric:gradient-convention]</a> yields $$\label{metric:gradient-bounds}
 |\nabla[-\log(1-t)]|_g^2=2t<2,
 \qquad
 |\nabla\psi|_g^2\le\frac{2|z|^2}{\lambda}\le\frac2\lambda.$$

<a id="metric:compact-containment"></a>
*Compact containment of metric balls.* Fix $p\in M_\phi$ and a finite radius $R\ge0$. Integrating <a href="#metric:gradient-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="metric:gradient-bounds">[metric:gradient-bounds]</a> along arbitrary piecewise smooth paths and taking the infimum of their lengths shows that $d_g(p,q)\le R$ implies $$-\log(1-t(q))\le a_R:=-\log(1-t(p))+\sqrt2R,
 \qquad
 \psi(q)\le b_R:=\psi(p)+\sqrt{2/\lambda}\,R.$$ This argument does not require a minimizing geodesic. Set $$\tau_R=1-e^{-a_R}<1,\qquad
 \rho_R=\sqrt{1-e^{-b_R}}<1.$$ Every such $q=(z,w)$ satisfies $t(q)\le\tau_R$ and $|z|\le\rho_R$. Let $m_R$ be the minimum of $\phi$ on the compact base ball $\{|z|\le\rho_R\}$. Then $$|w|^2=e^{-\phi(z)}t(q)\le e^{-m_R}\tau_R.$$ Consequently the metric ball is contained in $$\label{metric:compact-containment}
 K_R=\{(z,w):|z|\le\rho_R,
                  \ e^{\phi(z)}|w|^2\le\tau_R\}.$$ This set is closed and bounded in $\mathbb C^3$, hence compact, and the strict bounds $\rho_R<1$ and $\tau_R<1$ place it entirely inside $M_\phi$. In particular, a bounded-length curve cannot escape through the base boundary, through a fiber boundary, or through unbounded fiber coordinates. The zero section is an interior locus with the smooth positive metric <a href="#metric:axis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="metric:axis">[metric:axis]</a>.

The same compact-containment argument applies to sublevel sets of $\mathcal K_\lambda$. Both $\psi$ and $-\log(1-t)$ are nonnegative, so $\mathcal K_\lambda\le R$ implies $\psi\le R/\lambda$ and $-\log(1-t)\le R$. These bounds place the sublevel set in a compact set of the form <a href="#metric:compact-containment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="metric:compact-containment">[metric:compact-containment]</a>; the sublevel set is closed there, hence compact. Positivity of its complex Hessian was proved above, so $\mathcal K_\lambda$ is a strictly plurisubharmonic exhaustion.

*Geodesic completeness.* Suppose a unit-speed geodesic has a finite maximal forward time $T$. Its image lies in the compact set $K_T$. On this set the smooth positive metric has a uniform positive Euclidean lower bound, so the geodesic’s Euclidean velocity is bounded. The Christoffel symbols are also bounded on $K_T$, and the geodesic equation then bounds its Euclidean acceleration. Both position and velocity have limits as time tends to $T$, with limiting position in $K_T\subset M_\phi$. Local existence for the smooth geodesic equation extends the geodesic beyond $T$, a contradiction. The same argument in reverse time proves geodesic completeness. ◻

</details>

The hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#metric:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:transfer">3</a> imply $h\ge cI>0$, so Lemma <a href="#metric:complete" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:complete">21</a> applies for every $\lambda>0$ under consideration. It remains to choose $\lambda$ large enough for the two uniform real-sectional bounds. We do this in the next two sections, keeping the convention $g=2\operatorname{Re}\widehat g$ throughout.

<a id="sec:curvature"></a>
### Curvature on real two-planes

We retain the base potential and Hartogs metric of Proposition <a href="#metric:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:transfer">3</a>. Section <a href="#sec:pinching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pinching">9</a> will prove the two uniform sectional-curvature bounds. Here we compute the full curvature tensor and reduce its value on an arbitrary real two-plane to a scalar expression. In particular, the calculation includes real planes that are not complex lines.

Throughout this section, constants in tensor estimates depend only on the uniform centered-chart bounds for $h=(\phi_{i\bar j})$ in Proposition <a href="#metric:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:transfer">3</a>; the base dimension is two. We take $\lambda\ge1$. The base form remains $I=(\psi_{i\bar j})$, with $\psi=-\log(1-|z|^2)$.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### A pointwise holomorphic gauge and the tensor blocks

<a id="curv:gauge"></a>
Fix a point with $w\ne0$, choose centered ball coordinates at its base point, and choose a local branch of $\log w$. Put $$s=\log t=\phi+\log w+\overline{\log w},\qquad
 x=\frac{t}{1-t},\qquad q=x(1+x).$$ In these coordinates the centered base point is zero. Remove the first and pure second derivatives of the weight by the holomorphic fiber change $$P(z)=\sum_i\phi_i(0)z_i+
       \frac12\sum_{i,k}\phi_{ik}(0)z_i z_k,
 \qquad v=\log w+P(z).$$ More precisely, $s=v+\bar v+\widetilde\phi$, where $\widetilde\phi=\phi-P-\bar P$. At the point under consideration, $$\label{curv:gauge}
 s_i=s_{ik}=0,\qquad s_v=s_{\bar v}=1,\qquad
 s_{i\bar j}=h_{i\bar j}.$$ All higher derivatives of $s$ involving a vertical index vanish. Subtracting $P+\bar P$ changes neither $h$ nor its component derivatives in the centered base coordinates.

<a id="curv:symmetric-tensor"></a>
The metric at the point consequently has blocks $$\widehat g=\begin{pmatrix}G&0\\0&q\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad G=\lambda I+xh.$$ At the center, $I$ is the identity matrix. Normalize the vertical vector to $q^{-1/2}\partial_v$ and denote its index by $0$. Horizontal indices $i,j,k,l$ range over $1,2$. In this pointwise frame the metric is $G+V$, where $V$ is the Hermitian form whose only nonzero entry is $V_{0\bar0}=1$. Horizontal forms are extended by zero on the vertical direction. For any Hermitian form $P$, define $$\label{curv:symmetric-tensor}
 (S_P)_{a\bar b c\bar d}
   =P_{a\bar b}P_{c\bar d}+P_{a\bar d}P_{c\bar b}.$$

<a id="curv:coordinate-formula"></a>
Our complex curvature components use the convention $$\label{curv:coordinate-formula}
 R_{a\bar b c\bar d}
 =-\mathcal K_{ac\bar b\bar d}
   +\widehat g^{p\bar e}
       \mathcal K_{ac\bar e}\mathcal K_{p\bar b\bar d}.$$ Repeated indices in this formula are summed over all coordinates; repeated horizontal indices below are summed over $1,2$. The inverse coefficients satisfy $\sum_p\widehat g^{p\bar e}\widehat g_{p\bar j}
=\delta_{ej}$, so their numerical array is the transpose of the ordinary inverse of the displayed metric matrix. We relate <a href="#curv:coordinate-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:coordinate-formula">[curv:coordinate-formula]</a> to real sectional curvature below, with the fixed convention $g=2\operatorname{Re}\widehat g$.

<a id="stmt-curv-tensor"></a>
<div id="curv:tensor" class="proposition">

<a id="curv:tensor-identity"></a>
<a id="curv:four-horizontal-error"></a>
<a id="curv:three-horizontal-error"></a>
<a id="curv:component-errors"></a>
<a id="curv:D-bounds"></a>
**Proposition 22**. *In the frame just described, define the horizontal Hermitian form $$D=(1+2x)h-qhG^{-1}h.$$ The full curvature tensor is $$\label{curv:tensor-identity}
 R=-\lambda S_I-qS_h-S_V
    -(S_{D+V}-S_D-S_V)+T_4+T_3,$$ where $T_j$ has exactly $j$ horizontal indices. Its nonzero components, up to the Kähler symmetries and conjugation, are $$\begin{aligned}
 (T_4)_{i\bar j k\bar l}
   &=-x h_{i\bar j,k\bar l}
      +x^2G^{p\bar e}h_{i\bar e,k}h_{p\bar j,\bar l},
       \label{curv:four-horizontal-error}\\
 (T_3)_{i\bar j k\bar0}
   &=\sqrt q\bigl(-h_{i\bar j,k}
           +xG^{p\bar e}h_{i\bar e,k}h_{p\bar j}\bigr).
       \label{curv:three-horizontal-error}
\end{aligned}$$ Measured by the Euclidean component norms in this frame, uniformly for $\lambda\ge1$, $$\label{curv:component-errors}
 \|T_4\|\le C_4x,\qquad \|T_3\|\le C_3\sqrt q.$$ If $\lambda$ is at least the uniform upper bound for the eigenvalues of $h$ at centered-chart origins, then $$\label{curv:D-bounds}
 (1+x)h\le D\le(1+2x)h.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-curv-tensor"><summary>Proof of stmt-curv-tensor</summary>

*Proof.* Write $F(s)=-\log(1-e^s)$. Direct differentiation gives $$F'=x,\qquad F''=q,\qquad F'''=q(1+2x),\qquad
 F''''=q(1+6x+6x^2).$$ The centered-chart expression for $\psi$ differs from $-\log(1-|z|^2)$ by a pluriharmonic function and a constant. Its derivatives of type $(2,1)$ therefore vanish at the center, and its derivatives of type $(2,2)$ are $S_I$.

For brevity in the derivative tables, put $$A_{ik\bar j}=h_{i\bar j,k},\qquad
 B_{ik\bar j\bar l}=h_{i\bar j,k\bar l}.$$ Before normalizing the vertical vector, the only possibly nonzero third derivatives of type $(2,1)$, up to symmetry, are <a id="curv:third-derivatives"></a>$$\label{curv:third-derivatives}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal K_{ik\bar j}&=xA_{ik\bar j},\qquad
 \mathcal K_{iv\bar j}=qh_{i\bar j},\qquad
 \mathcal K_{vv\bar v}=q(1+2x).
 \end{aligned}$$ The fourth derivatives of type $(2,2)$, up to symmetry and conjugation, are <a id="curv:fourth-derivatives"></a>$$\label{curv:fourth-derivatives}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal K_{ik\bar j\bar l}
   &=\lambda(S_I)_{i\bar j k\bar l}
       +xB_{ik\bar j\bar l}+q(S_h)_{i\bar j k\bar l},\\
 \mathcal K_{ik\bar j\bar v}&=qA_{ik\bar j},\\
 \mathcal K_{iv\bar j\bar v}&=q(1+2x)h_{i\bar j},\\
 \mathcal K_{vv\bar v\bar v}&=q(1+6x+6x^2).
 \end{aligned}$$ To check that these lists are complete, apply the chain rule to $F(s)$ and use <a href="#curv:gauge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:gauge">[curv:gauge]</a>. In the four-horizontal derivative, the two pairings of mixed second derivatives of $s$ give $qS_h$; the third pairing contains $s_{ik}s_{\bar j\bar l}=0$. All other terms contain a vanished first or pure second horizontal derivative. In particular, $\mathcal K_{ik\bar v}=\mathcal K_{vv\bar j}=0$, $\mathcal K_{ik\bar v\bar v}=0$, and the fourth derivatives with one horizontal and three vertical indices vanish. No vanishing of pure third horizontal derivatives is needed.

<a id="curv:remaining-blocks"></a>
Substitution into <a href="#curv:coordinate-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:coordinate-formula">[curv:coordinate-formula]</a> gives <a href="#curv:four-horizontal-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:four-horizontal-error">[curv:four-horizontal-error]</a> and <a href="#curv:three-horizontal-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:three-horizontal-error">[curv:three-horizontal-error]</a>. For clarity, all remaining curvature types in the normalized frame are $$\label{curv:remaining-blocks}
 \begin{aligned}
 R_{i\bar j0\bar0}
   &=-(1+2x)h_{i\bar j}
       +q(hG^{-1}h)_{i\bar j}=-D_{i\bar j},\\
 R_{i\bar0k\bar0}&=0,\qquad
 R_{i\bar00\bar0}=0,\qquad
 R_{0\bar00\bar0}=-2.
 \end{aligned}$$ The last identity follows because its unnormalized value is $$-q(1+6x+6x^2)+q(1+2x)^2=-2q^2,$$ and the four vertical normalization factors multiply to $q^{-2}$. The tensors $S_V$ and $S_{D+V}-S_D-S_V$ give precisely the vertical and balanced two-horizontal blocks in <a href="#curv:remaining-blocks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:remaining-blocks">[curv:remaining-blocks]</a>. The Kähler symmetries and conjugation now account for every component, proving <a href="#curv:tensor-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:tensor-identity">[curv:tensor-identity]</a>.

The lower bound $h\ge cI$ implies $$\|G^{-1}\|\le\frac1{\lambda+cx},\qquad
 x^2\|G^{-1}\|\le\frac{x}{c}.$$ The assumed component-derivative bounds for $h$, applied to <a href="#curv:four-horizontal-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:four-horizontal-error">[curv:four-horizontal-error]</a> and <a href="#curv:three-horizontal-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:three-horizontal-error">[curv:three-horizontal-error]</a>, prove <a href="#curv:component-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:component-errors">[curv:component-errors]</a> with constants independent of $\lambda\ge1$ and $x>0$.
