Finally, $G=\lambda I+xh$ commutes with $h$ at the center. On an eigendirection of $h$ with eigenvalue $d$, the ratio of $D$ to $h$ is $$1+2x-\frac{x(1+x)d}{\lambda+xd}.$$ The subtracted fraction is nonnegative, and it is at most $x$ when $\lambda\ge d$. This proves <a href="#curv:D-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:D-bounds">[curv:D-bounds]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="curv:zero-section"></a>
Although the logarithmic fiber coordinate was used only for $w\ne0$, the tensor at the zero section can be checked directly in regular coordinates. A holomorphic fiber change $W=e^{P(z)+a}w$, with constant $a$ and a polynomial of degree at most two, makes the new weight have value zero and vanishing first and pure second derivatives at the base point. Its mixed Hessian remains $h$. Through total degree four, the terms relevant to the curvature are $$\lambda\psi+
 |W|^2\left(1+\sum_{i,j}h_{i\bar j}z_i\bar z_j\right)
 +\frac12|W|^4.$$ At the origin the metric is $\operatorname{diag}(\lambda I,1)$, all relevant third derivatives vanish, and direct differentiation gives $$\label{curv:zero-section}
 R_{i\bar j k\bar l}=-\lambda(S_I)_{i\bar j k\bar l},
 \qquad R_{i\bar j0\bar0}=-h_{i\bar j},
 \qquad R_{0\bar00\bar0}=-2,$$ with all other types zero. In the original fiber coordinate the normalized vertical vector is $e^{-\phi/2}\partial_w$. Thus the formulas above hold at the zero section as well by setting $x=q=0$, $G=\lambda I$, $D=h$, and $T_3=T_4=0$, and using this regular normalized vertical vector.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Real curvature and Gram determinants

Let $U_1,U_2$ be real linearly independent tangent vectors and let $u_1,u_2$ be their $(1,0)$ parts, so $U_j=u_j+\bar u_j$. For a Hermitian form $P$, linear in its first argument, define <a id="curv:gram-quantities"></a>$$\label{curv:gram-quantities}
 \begin{aligned}
 a_P&=P(u_1,u_1)P(u_2,u_2)
             -(\operatorname{Re}P(u_1,u_2))^2,\\
 b_P&=\operatorname{Im}P(u_1,u_2),\qquad
 m_P=a_{P+V}-a_P-a_V\quad\text{when $P$ is horizontal}.
 \end{aligned}$$ Thus $a_P$ is the determinant of the real Gram matrix for $\operatorname{Re}P$, $b_P$ is the imaginary Hermitian pairing, and $m_P$ is the cross term in that determinant after adding $V$. For the metric form $\widehat g$, the real squared area is $4a_{\widehat g}$ because $g=2\operatorname{Re}\widehat g$. For a tensor $T$ with the Kähler symmetries, also put $$\mathcal H_T=T_{u_1\bar u_1u_2\bar u_2}
       -\operatorname{Re}T_{u_1\bar u_2u_1\bar u_2}.$$ Here contraction with complex vectors has its usual multilinear meaning. These definitions allow the horizontal or vertical projection of the real plane to be degenerate.

<a id="stmt-curv-real-plane"></a>
<div id="curv:real-plane" class="lemma">

<a id="curv:sectional"></a>
<a id="curv:xi-definition"></a>
<a id="curv:xi-contraction"></a>
<a id="curv:S-contraction"></a>
**Lemma 23**. *For the metric convention $g=2\operatorname{Re}\widehat g$ and the curvature convention <a href="#curv:coordinate-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:coordinate-formula">[curv:coordinate-formula]</a>, $$\label{curv:sectional}
 K_g(\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb R}\{U_1,U_2\})
       =\frac{\mathcal H_R}{2a_{\widehat g}}.$$ Define the matrix $$\label{curv:xi-definition}
 \Xi^{a\bar b}=u_1^a\overline{u_2^b}
                   -u_2^a\overline{u_1^b}.$$ For every tensor $T$ with the Kähler symmetries and Hermitian conjugation symmetry, $$\label{curv:xi-contraction}
 \mathcal H_T=-\frac12
 T_{a\bar b c\bar d}\Xi^{a\bar b}\Xi^{c\bar d}.$$ For every Hermitian form $P$, $$\label{curv:S-contraction}
 \mathcal H_{S_P}=a_P+3b_P^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-curv-real-plane"><summary>Proof of stmt-curv-real-plane</summary>

*Proof.* The real squared area of the pair is $4a_{\widehat g}$, because $g(U_i,U_j)=2\operatorname{Re}\widehat g(u_i,u_j)$. In particular $a_{\widehat g}>0$ for a real linearly independent pair. Write $$A=R_{u_1\bar u_1u_2\bar u_2},\qquad
 B=R_{u_1\bar u_2u_1\bar u_2}.$$ The Kähler symmetries in the expansion on $U_j=u_j+\bar u_j$ give the real numerator $$R^{\mathbb R}(U_1,U_2,U_2,U_1)
      =A-B-\bar B+A=2\mathcal H_R.$$ Division by the real squared area proves <a href="#curv:sectional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:sectional">[curv:sectional]</a>. Expanding the right-hand side of <a href="#curv:xi-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:xi-contraction">[curv:xi-contraction]</a> gives the same expression $A-\operatorname{Re}B$ for $T$. Finally, if $c_P=P(u_1,u_2)$, direct contraction of <a href="#curv:symmetric-tensor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:symmetric-tensor">[curv:symmetric-tensor]</a> gives $$\mathcal H_{S_P}
   =P(u_1,u_1)P(u_2,u_2)+|c_P|^2-2\operatorname{Re}(c_P^2)
   =a_P+3b_P^2.$$ ◻

</details>

For example, the disk potential $-\log(1-|w|^2)$ has normalized complex curvature component $-2$ by <a href="#curv:remaining-blocks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:remaining-blocks">[curv:remaining-blocks]</a>. Taking $u_2=iu_1$ in <a href="#curv:sectional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:sectional">[curv:sectional]</a> gives real sectional curvature $-2$. This fixes the normalization also on complex lines.

<a id="stmt-curv-gram"></a>
<div id="curv:gram" class="lemma">

<a id="curv:mixed-gram"></a>
**Lemma 24**. *For a positive semidefinite Hermitian form $P$, $a_P\ge b_P^2$. For the vertical form $V$ one has $a_V=b_V^2$. If $P$ is horizontal and positive semidefinite, $m_P$ is nonnegative, linear and monotone in $P$, and $$\label{curv:mixed-gram}
 m_P\ge2\sqrt{a_Pa_V}.$$ When $a_Pa_V>0$, equality in <a href="#curv:mixed-gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:mixed-gram">[curv:mixed-gram]</a> holds exactly when the two real Gram matrices of the pair for $P$ and $V$ are proportional.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-curv-gram"><summary>Proof of stmt-curv-gram</summary>

*Proof.* Complex Cauchy–Schwarz gives $P(u_1,u_1)P(u_2,u_2)\ge|P(u_1,u_2)|^2$, proving $a_P\ge b_P^2$. Equality holds for $V$ because its complex rank is one. Let $A$ and $B$ be the two-by-two real Gram matrices with entries $\operatorname{Re}P(u_i,u_j)$ and $\operatorname{Re}V(u_i,u_j)$, respectively. Then $$a_P=\det A,\qquad a_V=\det B,\qquad
 m_P=\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{adj}(A)B).$$ For positive definite $A$, put $C=A^{-1/2}BA^{-1/2}$. The last expression equals $(\det A)\operatorname{tr}C$, whereas $\sqrt{a_Pa_V}=(\det A)\sqrt{\det C}$. The inequality $\operatorname{tr}C\ge2\sqrt{\det C}$ proves <a href="#curv:mixed-gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:mixed-gram">[curv:mixed-gram]</a>, and continuity gives the semidefinite case. If both determinants are positive, equality means that $C$ is a scalar matrix, which is exactly the claimed proportionality. The displayed formula for $m_P$ is linear in the entries of $P$. Its nonnegativity for positive semidefinite $P$ then also proves monotonicity. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Error terms and the curvature numerator

Decompose $u_j=(u_{j,H},u_{j,0})$ in the pointwise frame. Write $\Xi_{HH}$ for the horizontal two-by-two block of $\Xi$ and $\Xi_{H\bar0}$ for its horizontal-to-vertical column. The other mixed block is the negative conjugate transpose of this column. Write $\Xi_{\mathrm{mix}}$ for the matrix consisting of both mixed blocks, with all other entries zero. All norms of these blocks below are Euclidean Frobenius norms.

<a id="curv:xi-norms"></a>
Direct expansion of <a href="#curv:xi-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:xi-definition">[curv:xi-definition]</a> gives $$\label{curv:xi-norms}
 \begin{aligned}
 \|\Xi_{HH}\|^2&=2(a_I+b_I^2)\le4a_I,\\
 \|\Xi_{H\bar0}\|^2&=m_I+2b_Ib_V,\qquad
 \|\Xi_{\mathrm{mix}}\|^2=2(m_I+2b_Ib_V)\le4m_I.
 \end{aligned}$$ For the second identity, explicitly expand $\|u_{1,H}\overline{u_{2,0}}
      -u_{2,H}\overline{u_{1,0}}\|^2$. The last inequality follows from $|b_Ib_V|\le\sqrt{a_Ia_V}\le m_I/2$, by Lemma <a href="#curv:gram" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="curv:gram">24</a>.

<a id="stmt-curv-errors"></a>
<div id="curv:errors" class="lemma">

<a id="curv:contracted-errors"></a>
<a id="curv:mixed-error"></a>
**Lemma 25**. *For every real linearly independent tangent pair, $$\label{curv:contracted-errors}
 |\mathcal H_{T_4}|\le Cx a_I,\qquad
 |\mathcal H_{T_3}|\le C\sqrt q\,\sqrt{a_I m_I}.$$ More explicitly, the three-horizontal error satisfies $$\label{curv:mixed-error}
 |\mathcal H_{T_3}|
   \le C\sqrt q\,\|\Xi_{HH}\|\,
                         \|\Xi_{\mathrm{mix}}\|.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-curv-errors"><summary>Proof of stmt-curv-errors</summary>

*Proof.* In <a href="#curv:xi-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:xi-contraction">[curv:xi-contraction]</a>, the tensor $T_4$ pairs two horizontal blocks of $\Xi$. The tensor $T_3$ pairs one horizontal block and one mixed block, since it has exactly three horizontal indices. Cauchy–Schwarz and <a href="#curv:component-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:component-errors">[curv:component-errors]</a> give <a href="#curv:mixed-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:mixed-error">[curv:mixed-error]</a> and $|\mathcal H_{T_4}|\le Cx\|\Xi_{HH}\|^2$. Now use <a href="#curv:xi-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:xi-norms">[curv:xi-norms]</a>. These estimates remain valid when one of the projected real areas vanishes. ◻

</details>

<a id="curv:negative-numerator"></a>
We can now express the entire real curvature numerator. By <a href="#curv:S-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:S-contraction">[curv:S-contraction]</a> and $a_V=b_V^2$, $$\mathcal H_{S_V}=4a_V,\qquad
 \mathcal H_{S_{D+V}-S_D-S_V}=m_D+6b_Db_V.$$ Consequently <a href="#curv:tensor-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:tensor-identity">[curv:tensor-identity]</a> gives $$\label{curv:negative-numerator}
 \begin{aligned}
 -\mathcal H_R={}&\lambda(a_I+3b_I^2)+q(a_h+3b_h^2)
        +4a_V+m_D+6b_Db_V\\
       &-\mathcal H_{T_4}-\mathcal H_{T_3}.
 \end{aligned}$$ Its denominator in <a href="#curv:sectional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:sectional">[curv:sectional]</a> is positive for every real two-plane. It remains to prove that <a href="#curv:negative-numerator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:negative-numerator">[curv:negative-numerator]</a> is uniformly positive relative to that denominator for sufficiently large fixed $\lambda$, and that the quotient is also uniformly bounded above. The mixed term $6b_Db_V$ can have either sign; Section <a href="#sec:pinching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pinching">9</a> treats it together with the possible degeneration of the projected planes.

<a id="sec:pinching"></a>
### Uniform pinching on real two-planes

We now prove the curvature bounds in Proposition <a href="#metric:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:transfer">3</a>. The identity of Section <a href="#sec:curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:curvature">8</a> reduces the problem to finite-dimensional algebra. The limiting main expression is nonnegative, but it can vanish on certain real planes. We identify those planes and show that the error with three horizontal indices vanishes to the additional order needed there.

Throughout this section, horizontal forms are extended by zero on the vertical factor of $\mathbb C^2\oplus\mathbb C$. We retain $I,V,S_P,a_P,b_P,m_P,\Xi$ from Section <a href="#sec:curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:curvature">8</a>. Unless otherwise indicated, constants depend only on the fixed bounds $cI\le h\le CI$ and the component bounds for the error tensors.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### The mixed real-plane algebra

The leading expression below can vanish even when both projections of the real plane are nonzero. For an explicit algebraic model, take $h=I$, $E=2I$, and $u_1=(e_1,1)$, $u_2=(ie_1,-i)$, where $e_1=(1,0)\in\mathbb C^2$. Direct substitution gives $$a_h+3b_h^2+4a_V+m_E+6b_Eb_V=4+4+4-12=0,
 \qquad \Xi_{\mathrm{mix}}=0.$$ The horizontal and vertical projections have opposite complex orientations. The next lemma proves nonnegativity for all planes in the stated range of $E$ and shows that every zero has this mixed-block vanishing.

<a id="stmt-pinch-algebra"></a>
<div id="pinch:algebra" class="lemma">

<a id="pinch:square"></a>
**Lemma 26**. *Let $h$ be a positive definite horizontal Hermitian form, let $E$ be another horizontal Hermitian form with $E\ge h$, and let $u_1,u_2$ be real-linearly independent vectors in $\mathbb C^2\oplus\mathbb C$. Define $$P_h(E)=a_h+3b_h^2+4a_V+m_E+6b_Eb_V.$$ If $a_ha_V=0$, then $P_h(E)>0$. If $a_ha_V>0$, put $\tau=|b_h|/\sqrt{a_h}$. For every real $\beta$ such that $E\le(2-\beta)h$, $$\label{pinch:square}
 P_h(E)\ge
 \left(\sqrt{a_h+3b_h^2}-2\sqrt{a_V}\right)^2
 +2\beta(1+\tau)\sqrt{a_ha_V}.$$ In particular, $h\le E\le2h$ implies $P_h(E)\ge0$. If equality holds in this last conclusion, then $$a_h=a_V>0,\qquad |b_h|=\sqrt{a_h},\qquad b_Eb_V<0,$$ and both mixed blocks $\Xi^{i\bar0}$ and $\Xi^{0\bar i}$ vanish.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pinch-algebra"><summary>Proof of stmt-pinch-algebra</summary>

*Proof.* If $a_V=0$, then $b_V=0$, because the vertical factor has complex dimension one. If $a_h=0$, the two horizontal projections are linearly dependent over $\mathbb R$, since $h$ is positive definite. Their pairing under every Hermitian form is therefore real, so $b_E=0$. Thus $a_ha_V=0$ implies $b_Eb_V=0$, and $$P_h(E)\ge a_h+4a_V+m_h\ge a_{h+V}>0.$$ Here the last inequality uses the positive definiteness of $h+V$ and the real linear independence of the pair.

<a id="pinch:imaginary-bound"></a>
Suppose now that $A=a_h>0$ and $U=a_V>0$. The Hermitian Gram determinant gives $0\le\tau\le1$. Make a real change of basis in the plane so that its horizontal projections, denoted $u_H,v_H$, are real-orthonormal for $h$. Both ratios $|b_h|/\sqrt{a_h}$ and $|b_E|/\sqrt{a_h}$ are unchanged by this operation. The squared $h$-norms of $u_H+i v_H$ and $u_H-i v_H$ are $2(1+\tau)$ and $2(1-\tau)$, in some order. The absolute difference of their squared $E$-norms is $4|b_E|$ in the new basis. Comparing one norm from above and the other from below gives $$\label{pinch:imaginary-bound}
 \frac{|b_E|}{\sqrt A}
 \le\frac{(2-\beta)(1+\tau)-(1-\tau)}2
 =\frac{1+3\tau}{2}-\frac{\beta(1+\tau)}2
 \le\sqrt{1+3\tau^2}-\frac{\beta(1+\tau)}2.$$ For either order of the two norms, the displayed right side is an upper bound, since $2-\beta\ge1$. The last inequality follows from $$1+3\tau^2-\left(\frac{1+3\tau}{2}\right)^2
 =\frac34(1-\tau)^2;$$ it is strict unless $\tau=1$. This argument does not require $\beta\ge0$.

The mixed Gram inequality <a href="#curv:mixed-gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:mixed-gram">[curv:mixed-gram]</a> and the Hermitian Gram inequality yield $$m_E\ge2\sqrt{a_EU}\ge2|b_E|\sqrt U.$$ Since $U=b_V^2$, it follows that $$\begin{aligned}
 P_h(E)
 &\ge A+3b_h^2+4U-4|b_E|\sqrt U\\
 &\ge
 \left(\sqrt{A+3b_h^2}-2\sqrt U\right)^2
 +2\beta(1+\tau)\sqrt{AU},
\end{aligned}$$ which proves <a href="#pinch:square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pinch:square">[pinch:square]</a>.

It remains to examine equality when $h\le E\le2h$. The zero-area case already treated shows that $A,U>0$. Set $s=\sqrt{A+3b_h^2}$ and $B=|b_E|$. The inequalities above, with $\beta=0$, give $$P_h(E)\ge(s-2\sqrt U)^2+4\sqrt U(s-B)\ge0.$$ Equality forces $s=2\sqrt U$, $B=s$, and equality in the bounds used to reach this expression. The strictness in <a href="#pinch:imaginary-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pinch:imaginary-bound">[pinch:imaginary-bound]</a> gives $\tau=1$. Consequently $$U=A,\qquad B=2\sqrt A,\qquad b_Eb_V<0,\qquad
 m_E=2B\sqrt U.$$ Since $a_E\ge B^2$ and $m_E\ge2\sqrt{a_EU}$, these identities force $a_E=B^2$ and equality in the mixed Gram inequality. The horizontal real $E$-Gram matrix and the vertical real Gram matrix are therefore proportional.

In the real $h$-orthonormalized basis used above, $\tau=1$ says that the horizontal projections have the form $$u_H,\quad \sigma i u_H,\qquad \sigma\in\{1,-1\}.$$ Their real $E$-Gram matrix is a positive scalar multiple of the identity. Proportionality implies that the vertical projections have equal positive norms and real inner product zero. The sign $b_Eb_V<0$ selects the opposite complex orientation, so these projections have the form $$y,\quad-\sigma i y.$$ Direct substitution now gives $$\Xi^{i\bar0}
 =u_H^i\overline{-\sigma i y}-(\sigma i u_H^i)\bar y=0.$$ The other mixed block vanishes as well. Under any real change of basis in the plane, $\Xi$ is multiplied by the determinant of that change. The vanishing therefore holds in the original basis. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Uniform negativity for large $\lambda$

The next statement allows every error tensor with the specified bounds and component types. This uniformity will also give the compactness argument needed after $\lambda$ has been fixed.

<a id="stmt-pinch-positivity"></a>
<div id="pinch:positivity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27**. *Fix $0<c\le C<\infty$ and $0\le C_{\mathrm e}<\infty$. There is a finite $\lambda_0=\lambda_0(c,C,C_{\mathrm e})>0$ with the following property. Let $\lambda\ge\lambda_0$, $x\ge0$, and let $h$ be a horizontal Hermitian form with $cI\le h\le CI$. Put $$q=x(1+x),\qquad G=\lambda I+xh,\qquad
 D=(1+2x)h-qhG^{-1}h.$$ Let $T_4,T_3$ be tensors with the Kähler symmetries and reality condition, supported respectively on components with four and with three horizontal indices, and satisfying $$\|T_4\|\le C_{\mathrm e}x,\qquad
 \|T_3\|\le C_{\mathrm e}\sqrt q.$$ Define $$R=-\lambda S_I-qS_h-S_V-(S_{D+V}-S_D-S_V)+T_4+T_3.$$ Then $\mathcal H_R<0$ on every real two-plane.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pinch-positivity"><summary>Proof of stmt-pinch-positivity</summary>

<a id="pinch:leading-numerator"></a>
*Proof.* Increase the prospective threshold so that $\lambda\ge\max(1,C)$. Then <a href="#curv:D-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:D-bounds">[curv:D-bounds]</a> gives $$(1+x)h\le D\le(1+2x)h.$$ If the lemma were false, there would be a sequence $\lambda\to\infty$ of permitted data and real two-planes for which $F=-\mathcal H_R\le0$. We suppress the sequence index. By <a href="#curv:negative-numerator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:negative-numerator">[curv:negative-numerator]</a>, $$\label{pinch:leading-numerator}
 F=\lambda(a_I+3b_I^2)+q(a_h+3b_h^2)+4a_V
 +m_D+6b_Db_V-\mathcal H_{T_4}-\mathcal H_{T_3}.$$ The contracted estimates <a href="#curv:contracted-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:contracted-errors">[curv:contracted-errors]</a> apply to the allowed error tensors by their component types and symmetries.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
###### The regime $q/\lambda\to0$.

Suppose first that a subsequence has this property. Monotonicity and linearity of the mixed Gram term give $m_D\ge c(1+x)m_I$, while $$|b_D|\le\sqrt{a_D}\le C'(1+x)\sqrt{a_I}.$$ Discarding nonnegative terms in <a href="#pinch:leading-numerator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pinch:leading-numerator">[pinch:leading-numerator]</a>, we get $$F\ge\lambda a_I+4a_V+c(1+x)m_I
 -C'\bigl((1+x)\sqrt{a_Ia_V}+x a_I+
             \sqrt q\,\sqrt{a_Im_I}\bigr).$$ Absorb the first product into $2a_V$, and the last into $\frac c2(1+x)m_I$. Since $q/(1+x)=x$, this gives $$F\ge[\lambda-C''((1+x)^2+x)]a_I+
 2a_V+\frac c2(1+x)m_I.$$ Here $(1+x)^2+x=O(1+q)=o(\lambda)$, because $\lambda\to\infty$. Every coefficient is eventually positive, whereas $a_I+a_V+m_I=a_{I+V}>0$. This contradicts $F\le0$.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
###### The regime with $\lambda/q$ bounded.

If the preceding regime has no subsequence, we may pass to one on which $q/\lambda$ is bounded below by a positive constant. Then $\lambda/q$ is bounded and $x\to\infty$. The term $qS_h$ has four horizontal slots, whereas the vertical term $S_V$ already has coefficient one. To balance these two blocks, multiply horizontal vector components by $q^{1/4}$. In the resulting plane choose a real-orthonormal pair for $\operatorname{Re}(I+V)$. The inverse transformation and a real basis change give a basis of the original plane; the latter change multiplies $F$ by a positive factor. Thus its sign is unchanged. The rescaled pairs belong to a fixed compact Stiefel manifold, and their quantities satisfy $a_{I+V}=1$.

<a id="pinch:rescaled-leading"></a>
<a id="pinch:rescaled-errors"></a>
In the rescaled variables, the leading part of <a href="#pinch:leading-numerator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pinch:leading-numerator">[pinch:leading-numerator]</a> is $$\label{pinch:rescaled-leading}
 L_x=\frac{\lambda}{q}(a_I+3b_I^2)+P_h(E),
 \qquad E=\frac D{\sqrt q}.$$ The transformed tensors, denoted $\widetilde T_4,\widetilde T_3$, satisfy $$\label{pinch:rescaled-errors}
 \|\widetilde T_4\|\le C'\frac{x}{q}=O(x^{-1}),
 \qquad
 \|\widetilde T_3\|\le C'q^{-1/4}=O(x^{-1/2}).$$ Indeed, each horizontal slot supplies a factor $q^{-1/4}$.

<a id="pinch:E-bounds"></a>
The lower bound on $D$ gives $E\ge h$. For an eigenvalue $d\in[c,C]$ of $h$, the corresponding ratio is $$\frac Eh=\frac{1+2x}{\sqrt q}
           -\frac{\sqrt q\,d}{\lambda+xd}.$$ Since $$\frac{1+2x}{\sqrt q}=\sqrt{4+\frac1q}
 \le2+\frac{C_1}{x^2},
 \qquad
 \frac{\sqrt q\,d}{\lambda+xd}
 \ge c_1\frac{x}{\lambda+x},$$ we have the uniform bound $$\label{pinch:E-bounds}
 h\le E\le
 \left(2+\frac{C_1}{x^2}
          -c_1\frac{x}{\lambda+x}\right)h.$$ The constants $c_1>0,C_1<\infty$ depend only on $c,C$.

Pass to a subsequence on which $h,E,\lambda/q$, and the normalized pair converge. The limiting forms satisfy $h\le E\le2h$. The error contributions tend to zero by <a href="#pinch:rescaled-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pinch:rescaled-errors">[pinch:rescaled-errors]</a>. Lemma <a href="#pinch:algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pinch:algebra">26</a> shows that the two limiting summands in <a href="#pinch:rescaled-leading" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pinch:rescaled-leading">[pinch:rescaled-leading]</a> are nonnegative. Since $F\le0$, both must vanish. In particular $P_h(E)=0$ at the limit, so $a_h,a_V>0$ there and the mixed blocks of the limiting $\Xi$ vanish. The comparison $$c^2a_I\le a_h\le C^2a_I$$ follows from the corresponding real Gram-matrix bounds. Hence $a_I,a_h,a_V$ are bounded below by positive constants along this subsequence.

<a id="pinch:vanishing-error"></a>
We now improve the error estimate on these particular planes. By the contraction formula <a href="#curv:xi-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:xi-contraction">[curv:xi-contraction]</a>, a tensor with exactly three horizontal indices can pair only one horizontal-horizontal block with one mixed block. Thus $$\label{pinch:vanishing-error}
 |\mathcal H_{\widetilde T_3}|
 \le C'q^{-1/4}\|\Xi_{HH}\|\,\|\Xi_{\mathrm{mix}}\|
 =o(x^{-1/2}).$$ Here $\Xi_{HH}$ denotes the block $(\Xi^{i\bar j})$, and $\Xi_{\mathrm{mix}}$ comprises the blocks $(\Xi^{i\bar0})$ and $(\Xi^{0\bar i})$. The first block is bounded because the normalized pairs form a compact set, and the mixed blocks tend to zero by the equality case just proved. The explicit tensor factor $q^{-1/4}$ is what makes this an $o(x^{-1/2})$ estimate for the evaluated contribution. No rate of convergence of the planes is being assumed.

<a id="pinch:positive-gap"></a>
Apply <a href="#pinch:square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pinch:square">[pinch:square]</a> before taking the limit, with $$\beta=c_1\frac{x}{\lambda+x}-\frac{C_1}{x^2}.$$ This choice is permitted by <a href="#pinch:E-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pinch:E-bounds">[pinch:E-bounds]</a>, including when $\beta<0$. The positive lower bounds for $a_I,a_h,a_V$, and boundedness of the normalized pairs, imply $$\label{pinch:positive-gap}
 L_x\ge c_2\left(\frac{\lambda}{q}
                  +\frac{x}{\lambda+x}\right)-\frac{C_2}{x^2}$$ along the subsequence, for some $c_2>0,C_2<\infty$. For $x\ge1$, if $\lambda\ge x^{3/2}$, then $$\frac{\lambda}{q}\ge\frac{x^{3/2}}{2x^2}
 =\frac1{2\sqrt x}.$$ If instead $\lambda\le x^{3/2}$, then $$\frac{x}{\lambda+x}\ge\frac1{\sqrt x+1}
 \ge\frac1{2\sqrt x}.$$ Thus <a href="#pinch:positive-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pinch:positive-gap">[pinch:positive-gap]</a> is bounded below by a positive constant times $x^{-1/2}$ for large $x$. The evaluated $\widetilde T_4$ contribution is $O(x^{-1})=o(x^{-1/2})$, and <a href="#pinch:vanishing-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pinch:vanishing-error">[pinch:vanishing-error]</a> gives the same little-o comparison for $\widetilde T_3$. It follows that $F>0$ eventually, a contradiction.

The two regimes exhaust the possible failure sequences. This proves the asserted finite threshold $\lambda_0$, uniformly over all permitted matrices, error tensors, and real planes. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Both curvature bounds at a fixed parameter

<details id="proof-stmt-pinch-positivity-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-pinch-positivity</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#metric:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:transfer">3</a>.* The geometric conclusions concerning the domain and completeness were proved in Lemma <a href="#metric:complete" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:complete">21</a>. It remains to obtain both uniform real sectional-curvature bounds. By Proposition <a href="#curv:tensor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="curv:tensor">22</a>, the errors of the actual metric have the types and bounds in Lemma <a href="#pinch:positivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pinch:positivity">27</a>, with $C_{\mathrm e}$ depending only on the chart bounds of Proposition <a href="#metric:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:transfer">3</a>. Choose any finite $\lambda\ge\lambda_0$. Then $-\mathcal H_R>0$ for every point and every real two-plane. We show that the sectional-curvature quotient is bounded away from zero and from negative infinity.

First restrict $x$ to a bounded interval $[0,X]$. Normalize the pairs for $\operatorname{Re}(I+V)$. The data consisting of $x$, the matrix $h$, the component arrays of $T_4,T_3$, and the normalized pair range over a closed bounded subset of a finite-dimensional space: impose $cI\le h\le CI$, the stated component types and symmetries, and the closed norm bounds $C_{\mathrm e}x,C_{\mathrm e}\sqrt q$. This set is compact. The formulas for $G,D,R$ are continuous on it. Since Lemma <a href="#pinch:positivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pinch:positivity">27</a> applies to this entire set, $-\mathcal H_R$ has a strictly positive minimum and a finite maximum. The metric $G+V$ is uniformly positive definite and bounded there, so $a_{G+V}$ is bounded above and away from zero. Formula <a href="#curv:sectional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:sectional">[curv:sectional]</a> therefore gives both a strictly negative upper curvature bound and a finite lower curvature bound on $0\le x\le X$.

<a id="pinch:metric-limit"></a>
<a id="pinch:D-limit"></a>
<a id="pinch:tensor-limit"></a>
To cover the end $x\to\infty$, use the horizontal rescaling from the preceding proof. Here $\lambda$ remains fixed: the mixed form $D/\sqrt q$ will converge to $h$, rather than to an arbitrary form between $h$ and $2h$ as allowed in the preceding contradiction argument. The rescaled Hermitian metric is $$\widetilde g_x=G/\sqrt q+V.$$ For the fixed finite $\lambda$, $$\label{pinch:metric-limit}
 \frac G{\sqrt q}-h
 =\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt q}I+
   \left(\frac{x}{\sqrt q}-1\right)h
 =O_\lambda(x^{-1})$$ uniformly in the permitted data. Because $G$ commutes with $h$, the formula for $D$ also gives $$D=xh+\lambda(1+x)hG^{-1}.$$ Consequently $$\label{pinch:D-limit}
 \frac D{\sqrt q}-h
 =\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt q}-1\right)h+
   \frac{\lambda(1+x)}{\sqrt q}hG^{-1}
 =O_\lambda(x^{-1}).$$ The rescaled tensor identity now implies $$\label{pinch:tensor-limit}
 \widetilde R_x=-S_{h+V}+O_\lambda(x^{-1/2})$$ in component norm, uniformly over all allowed data. Indeed, the term $\lambda S_I$ is divided by $q$, the term $qS_h$ becomes exactly $S_h$, the mixed term converges by <a href="#pinch:D-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pinch:D-limit">[pinch:D-limit]</a>, and the error tensors satisfy <a href="#pinch:rescaled-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pinch:rescaled-errors">[pinch:rescaled-errors]</a>.

For the limiting metric $h+V$ and tensor $-S_{h+V}$, $$K=-\frac12\left(1+
     3\frac{b_{h+V}^2}{a_{h+V}}\right)
 \in[-2,-1/2].$$ For an $(I+V)$-real-orthonormal pair, $a_{h+V}\ge\min(c,1)^2$, so these area denominators are uniformly bounded away from zero. The forms $h+V$ also range over a compact bounded family. The metric and tensor estimates <a href="#pinch:metric-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pinch:metric-limit">[pinch:metric-limit]</a>–<a href="#pinch:tensor-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pinch:tensor-limit">[pinch:tensor-limit]</a> therefore imply uniform convergence of the corresponding sectional-curvature quotients over all real planes. For sufficiently large $X_\lambda<\infty$, they give, for example, $$-3\le K_g\le-1/4\qquad(x\ge X_\lambda).$$ Combining this with the compact-range bounds at $X=X_\lambda$ produces finite constants $0<A\le B$ for which $$-B\le K_g(\sigma)\le-A<0$$ for every point and every real two-plane. The tensor identity at $x=0$, given in <a href="#curv:zero-section" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curv:zero-section">[curv:zero-section]</a>, is computed in a smooth normalized fiber frame; equivalently the same bounds extend to the zero section by smoothness of the metric. This completes Proposition <a href="#metric:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:transfer">3</a>. ◻

</details>
